Kuczynski 001 — PASS
Question: Does logic require the existence of non-spatiotemporal entities? Give the property-based argument.
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 8
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Causality; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology
Word count: 234

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. Logic requires non-spatiotemporal entities, and the property-based argument shows why.

Start with the inference: "Bob and Sally are both intelligent and kind." This is rudimentary and indisputably correct. But to validate it, "it is necessary to grant the existence of a property that Bob and Sally have in common." That property is not a thing you can bump into.

Here is the mechanism. Instances of properties exist in space-time. "Bob and Sally, both instances of many properties, exist in space and time." But "although you may encounter many instances of intelligence, you'll never encounter intelligence per se and it would make no sense to assign any spatiotemporal location to it." The instance is located; the property is not.

So the inference from "Bob and Sally are both intelligent" to "there exists some property that Bob and Sally have in common" forces us to "posit some entity whose existence one would otherwise deny." That is ontologizing, and it is unavoidable.

The same holds for uninstantiated properties. Consider triangularity. "Were we to say that it doesn't exist, we'd be saying that, since there are no instances of the property of triangularity, that property doesn't exist." That is absurd. So "the property of having a perfectly triangular shape exists even though nothing has it."

Hence: "Intelligence itself is not in space-time." Logic traffics in such entities. To validate even the simplest inference, we must grant them.

[Causality] Nothing non-spatiotemporal can be a cause. There are causes only where there are changes, and changes happen in time. So universals, if they exist, are causally impotent.
[Causality] Contrary to what Aristotle thought, dependence-relations don’t hold between objects. They hold between events or between statements, depending on whether they’re of a causal or a logical nature.
[Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence] In conclusion, unless it’s conceded that properties exist, there is no way to account for the fact that there are principled relationships of any kind.

3. Plato snores.
At this point in time, Plato doesn’t exist. So if he’s a (necessary) constituent of 3—if, in other words, 3 exists only if Plato does—then 3 doesn’t exist.
[Causality] It thus seems to me that counterfactuals are just disguised causal or logical assertions and that, consequently, alternative worlds have no place in any decent analysis of them.

[Causality] We don’t observe electrons or quarks, but we have good reason to believe them to exist. How are causal ties different? Given that it’s methodologically permissible to posit unobservable viruses and particles, why isn’t it equally permissible to posit unobservable causal ties?
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] It is because of its causal properties that T is a tree; it isn't because of its effects on anyone's ideation.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Given any two logically equivalent truths, no matter how non-obvious it is that they are logically equivalent, nothing could confirm the one but disconfirm the other; and if the a given datum D (dis) confirmed the one to a given degree, that datum would necessarily (dis) confirm the other to that same degree.
[Causality] In fact, the concepts of space and time are to be understood in terms of the concept of causality, and Hume’s analysis is viciously circular for this reason.
[Causality] Events have causal roots. Two worlds cannot differ with respect to a single event unless they differ with respect to all of the causal antecedents of those events.

Kuczynski 002 — PASS
Question: What is a property, and why is a property not identical with any of its instances?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 11
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Part II; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Causality
Word count: 292

EXACT ANSWER
A property is a way that things can be. Equivalently, a property is a possibility.

That is the whole of it. The property of being a pink elephant is not some pink elephant in the sky; it is "just a way something would have to be to be a pink elephant." And for there to be no instances of that property is simply for nothing to be that way.

Properties can also be thought of as conditions. "The property of being a pink elephant is a condition a thing must fulfill in order to be a pink elephant." Mary's condition on a suitor—genius, always friendly, never sleeps, a billion dollars—is unfulfilled by anyone, yet the condition exists. So "properties that aren't instantiated are just unfulfilled conditions."

Now the identity question. A property is not identical with its instances. The proof is modal. "The property of being a pink elephant exists, but is currently uninstantiated. If there were to come into being a single pink elephant, which promptly died, the property wouldn't go out of existence." But if the property were identical with its instances, "it would go out of existence the moment its one instance did."

The general lesson: "properties tend to be modally elastic, whereas sets are modally frozen." A property can generate different instances in different worlds; the set of its instances cannot.

This is why scattered-object reductions fail. Some try to identify the property of being a rock with a scattered object made of all rocks. But that object's boundary shifts constantly, while the property does not. "Analogous reasoning refutes any attempt to identify any property with any spatiotemporal entity."

Properties are not in space-time. Search the universe and you will find at most their instances—never the property itself.

[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] So propositions are functions, and those functions are individuated by their intensions, not their extensions.
[Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence] Properties lack causal powers. For this reason, they can serve no explanatory function.
[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] Propositions are intensions (though not all intensions are propositions—e.g., the propositional function x is a human being is not a proposition, even though it is an intension).

Kuczynski 003 — PASS
Question: Can a non-spatiotemporal entity have causal powers?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 11
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 279

EXACT ANSWER
No. A non-spatiotemporal entity cannot have causal powers. This is not a limitation I lament—it is a consequence of what causation is.

To be a cause is to induce change. And change is spatiotemporal through and through. As I've argued, "Causes are necessarily in space and time. To be a cause is to induce change. Changes and their causes are ipso facto in space-time."

This is why properties, on my Platonic view, lack causal powers—and why that is no defect. "Properties do indeed lack causal powers." But the inference so many draw from this—that properties are therefore explanatory deadwood—is simply invalid. "Not all explanation is causal explanation. Some explanation is what we call analytic or conceptual."

Consider the nomic relations that structure the world. "While it is a matter of intense debate what nomic relations are, it is pretty clear they hold only among occupants of the space-time manifold." My pounding the table is nomically connected to the table's breaking. My not destroying Jupiter is not nomically related to anything. "My not destroying Jupiter is nowhere in that tapestry, and therefore isn't fit to stand in nomic relations to anything."

The same holds for properties. "Ways things can be are not spatiotemporal. A way that something can be is simply a possibility as to how it can be, and such a possibility obviously isn't a constituent of space-time, even if the object in question is."

So the demand that properties earn their keep by pushing things around is confused. "To explain something is to show how it came about"—but that is only one species of explanation. Properties explain by being the ways things are, not by making things happen.

Kuczynski 004 — PASS
Question: Why does the existence of properties follow from meaningful predication?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Part II; Causality; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 271

EXACT ANSWER
The existence of properties follows from meaningful predication because the logical behavior of predicate terms demands referents. When I say "x is red," I commit myself to something more than a syntactic arrangement of marks.

Consider the inference structure. "If I know that: (1) x is red is true, then I can logically infer (2) There exists some color or other that x has." This inference is not trivial. It tracks a genuine ontological commitment. "This inference involves affirming the existence of the thing meant by the term 'red.'"

The same pattern recurs across predicates. "Given: (3) Fred is smart but Larry is not, I can infer that (4) there exists some property that Fred has that Larry lacks." The inference works because "smart" denotes something — a property — that Fred possesses and Larry does not.

Now contrast this with genuinely syncategorematic terms. "Words like 'of' don't behave in a comparable manner." From "There is a picture of Paul on the mantelpiece," I cannot infer the existence of some entity meant by "of." The word does no referential work. It is purely structural.

This asymmetry is decisive. "Expressions like 'red' and 'the property of redness' have very different logical properties from expressions that are known to be syncategorematic." Predicates license existential generalizations; syncategorematic terms do not.

Therefore, "it is not easy to make a case that words like 'smart' and 'red'—words that denote properties—lack referents and are really to be thought of on the model of purely syntactic terms like 'of.'" The inferential role of predicates commits us to properties. Meaningful predication is not merely grammatical; it is ontologically loaded.

Kuczynski 005 — PASS
Question: What are propositions?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Part II; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 266

EXACT ANSWER
Propositions are sets of properties. That is my core thesis. "By a 'proposition,' I mean what is meant by an utterance of a sentence." When I point at Smith and say "that man is a scoundrel," the meaning of my utterance is a proposition.

A proposition is true just in case the properties that are members of it are instantiated. "Roughly, truth is instantiatedness." So what it is for a proposition to be true is for the members of the set with which it is identical to be jointly instantiated.

Propositions are not sentences. "Propositions are not themselves sentences. That is why different sentences (e.g., 'schnee ist weiss' and 'snow is white') can express the same proposition." Nor are they psychological entities. "Propositions are not psychological entities; they are mind-independent entities." The believing is mental; the believed is not.

I also define them functionally. "We thus have three different definitions of 'proposition': (i) thing meant by a sentence; (ii) thing towards which one can have an attitude (e.g., belief, disbelief, fear, hope); (iii) non-derivative bearer of truth or falsity." Sentences and beliefs are only derivatively true; propositions are the non-derivative bearers.

Structurally, propositions have discrete parts. "It follows that propositions consist of discrete parts; it also follows that those discrete parts correspond to discrete parts of the sentences that express them." They are digital structures, not amorphous blobs.

One more consequence. "Propositions are non-spatiotemporal entities. But they too are properties." And since properties are not mind-independent, neither are propositions. They are not human creations—there are infinitely many, and we could not have directly created them all.

Kuczynski 006 — PASS
Question: What makes a proposition true?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 302

EXACT ANSWER
A proposition is true just in case the properties that are members of it are instantiated. That is the whole of it. Truth is instantiatedness.

I identify propositions with sets of properties. "Propositions are sets of properties; and a proposition is true just in case the properties that are members of it are instantiated—so what it is for a proposition to be true is for the members of the set with which it is identical to be jointly instantiated." Truth, then, is not a further property tacked onto a proposition. It is the proposition's members being instantiated.

Consider the proposition that Tommy is smoking. On my account, it is a set of properties—being identical with Tommy, being a smoker, and so on. The proposition is true iff each of those properties is instantiated. Nothing more is required. "Roughly, truth is instantiatedness."

This is why I say a proposition's being true is identical with its being instantiated. "Propositions are properties and truth is instantiatedness." The world's being a certain way just is its having a certain property; and the world's being that way is what it is for the corresponding proposition to be true.

Note what follows. Truth is not a human creation. "The world was a certain way before we were around; it will be a certain way after we're gone." So certain propositions were true before us and will be true after us. Sentences are our artifacts; propositions are not. "Sentences, on the other hand, are human creations. They wouldn't be around if it weren't for us."

And the truth of a contingent proposition supervenes on something more basic. "A contingent proposition is never just true—it is true in virtue of some fact about the basic constituents of the world." The rippling of the quantum. That is what makes it true.

[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] In order for anyone to say anything, words must have meanings, and there must therefore be true propositions saying what those meanings are.

[Causality] Thus, we are warranted in affirming counterfactuals only where we already have explanations.

Kuczynski 007 — PASS
Question: Can truth be reduced to correspondence between a proposition and a fact?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 4
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Part II; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 246

EXACT ANSWER
Truth is correspondence, but not between a proposition and a fact—between a proposition and the facts. The distinction matters enormously.

I've argued that "for a proposition to be true is for it to fit the facts." That's the obvious answer, though imprecise. But notice what I'm NOT saying. I'm not saying truth is a relation between a proposition and some single fact that mirrors it. Propositions don't encode anything—sentences do. "For a sentence to be 'true' is for it to encode a true proposition. But this isn't what it is for a proposition to be true, since propositions don't encode anything."

This is where the reductionist picture collapses. The term "statement" is ambiguous—"it has three distinct meanings. Sometimes it refers to propositions, sometimes it refers to the sentences used to affirm them, and sometimes it refers to the act of using a sentence to affirm a proposition." Confuse these and you'll think truth is a simple two-place relation. It isn't.

Wittgenstein didn't countenance propositions at all, and this "had a hand in his erroneously believing that philosophy is the analysis of sentences, when the truth is that philosophy is the analysis of propositions." If you eliminate propositions, you can't even state the correspondence theory properly.

So: truth IS correspondence to the facts. But the correspondence is between propositions and reality—not between sentences and facts, not between statements-as-acts and facts. Get the relata right, and the theory works. Get them wrong, and you're doing linguistics, not philosophy.

Kuczynski 008 — PASS
Question: Why are propositions not sentences or mental events?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 13
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Part II; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Causality; Do We Think in Words?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 224

EXACT ANSWER
Propositions are not sentences because sentences are human artifacts while propositions are not. "Sentences, on the other hand, are human creations. They wouldn't be around if it weren't for us." The proposition that snow is white existed before anyone wrote "snow is white" or "schnee ist weiss." That is why "different sentences (e.g., 'schnee ist weiss' and 'snow is white') can express the same proposition."

Propositions are not mental events because the thing believed is not the believing. "The believing is mental; the believed is not." When Smith believes the inventor of bifocals is six feet tall, his belief is a psychological condition—but the object of that belief is not. "Smith's having that belief is indeed a psychological condition. But the thing believed is not."

The proof is that others can grasp the same thing. "Whatever it is, that thing is something that others grasp." If the object of belief were Smith's private mental state, no one else could believe the same thing. But they can. So the thing believed must be mind-independent.

This is why I say propositions are properties. "Propositions are properties and truth is instantiatedness." A proposition is true when the world has a certain property. The world's being a certain way is not a sentence and not a mental event—it is a fact about how things stand, independent of us.

Kuczynski 009 — PASS
Question: What is wrong with treating possible worlds as the basis of counterfactual truth?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 274

EXACT ANSWER
The central problem is epistemic: we cannot sense-perceive other worlds, so any knowledge we have of them is parasitic on our knowledge of counterfactual truths. This makes the analysis circular.

As I've argued, "We obviously can’t see or otherwise sense-perceive what is going on in other worlds; nor can we can sense-perceive evidence of what is going on in such worlds." So even if a counterfactual is true just in case some x-free world validates it, "our knowledge of (i) has nothing to do with other worlds." We know counterfactuals because we know facts in our world—not because we've inspected alternative ones.

Lewis himself concedes we don't tour other worlds. We construct them by "employing a principle of recombination"—applying modal principles we already possess. But then "the worlds answer to us, not us to them." The tail wags the dog.

There's a deeper failure. To validate a counterfactual like "if Smith had not shot Jones, Jones would have lived," Lewis must posit a miracle in the nearest world. But "if a miracle is needed to validate MLASK, then a miracle will be needed to validate any counterfactual." And since Smith's shooting involves "trillions upon trillions of mass-energy displacements," we'd need trillions of miracles. As I put it, "A theory that only doctored data supports is a bad theory, and Lewis’ analysis of counterfactuals is untenable."

Finally, primitive possible-worlds semantics cannot distinguish analytically equivalent propositions. "The class of worlds where 1 + 1 = 2 is identical with the class of worlds triangles have three sides." Yet those propositions are distinct—you can think one without the other.

Other worlds are useless for validating modal knowledge.

Kuczynski 010 — PASS
Question: How should counterfactual conditionals be understood?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 13
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Part II; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 272

EXACT ANSWER
Counterfactuals are disguised causal or logical assertions. They are not reports about other universes.

I find the possible-worlds analysis "completely preposterous." We have no independent access to other worlds. "We don't know anything about other universes, let alone whether Smith and Kennedy exist in them or, therefore, what their situations there are." Whatever we seem to know about such universes is parasitic on our grasp of counterfactual truths, not the reverse. "Our knowledge of counterfactuals precedes our knowledge of other worlds."

The correct analysis treats them as "grammatical variants of garden variety causal or logical statements." Take K: "Kennedy's assassination prevented an otherwise reelection bound Kennedy from being reelected." Take S: "Anything that is half the height of something that is six-feet tall is three-feet tall."

So "If Smith hadn't fallen off the building, he wouldn't have died" is equivalent to "Falling off the building caused an otherwise unthreatened Smith to die." And "If 6-feet tall Smith were half his current height, he'd be 3-feet tall" is equivalent to "Smith is 6-feet tall entails anything that is 3-feet tall is half Smith's height."

Notice the difference: "SH is analytically true, whereas SB is non-analytically true." One registers a logical entailment; the other registers a causal fact.

This exposes the vicious circularity of the counterfactual analysis of causation. That analysis assumes counterfactual truth is prior to causality. But counterfactual truth is itself "to be understood in terms of causality." So the analysis is "guilty of vicious circularity."

We know counterfactuals "by examining the causal and logical structure of our world, not other worlds." There is no reason to posit those worlds at all.

Kuczynski 011 — PASS
Question: Are laws of nature invariant with respect to every region of space?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 249

EXACT ANSWER
No. Not all laws of nature need to be spatiotemporally invariant.

Some philosophers of science have taken the term "general principle" to mean "spatiotemporally invariant principle," and they have thus taken the position that the laws of nature must be spatiotemporally invariant. I reject that inference. A principle or law is spatiotemporally invariant if, given any two regions of space-time, it holds no less for the one than the other. But many of the dependence-relations on which scientists depend are undefined, and therefore neither true nor false, for almost all regions of space-time.

Consider what a law actually does. For it to be a law of nature that instances of phi bring about instances of psi is not for each and every phi to be followed by an instance of psi. There could be such a law even if no phi ever brought about any psi. For there to be such a law is for instances of phi to be dispositive, for reasons of a relatively non-context-specific nature, of the occurrence of instances of psi.

That is the key. Laws require no perfect concomitance. They require dispositions of a relatively non-context-specific kind.

So invariance across every region of space-time is the wrong criterion. What matters is whether a dependence-relation is principled and defeasible, not whether it holds uniformly everywhere.

As long as there is a need for science, principled but defeasible generalizations are to be co-categorized with principled, non-defeasible generalizations. They are to be categorized as laws of nature.

[Causality] Bottom line: once it is realized that accidental generalizations don’t receive support from their instances, this being the prerogative of principled generalizations, it isn’t possible for Hempel’s paradox even to arise.
[Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism] Sellars has shown that in non-standard conditions, one must rely (however unconsciously) on background knowledge if one is to learn, through one’s senses, how things are in the external world.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Rules, by definition, are never implicit. They must either be stated explicitly, or (as with derivative semantic rules) they must be logical consequences of explicitly stated rules.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] The proposition, supposing there to be one, affirmed by a given sentence-token is a function of what its parts refer to.

Kuczynski 012 — PASS
Question: What distinguishes a law of nature from an accidental regularity?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 287

EXACT ANSWER
The difference is not statistical. It is dispositional and rooted in inherent properties.

A law of nature does not require perfect correlation. As I argue, "Laws of nature are not perfect concomitances: for it to be a law of nature that instances of phi bring about instances of psi is not for each and every phi to be followed by an instance of psi." There could be such a law "even if no phi ever brought about any psi." What makes it a law is that "instances of phi [are] dispositive, for reasons of a relatively non-context-specific nature, of the occurrence of instances of psi."

Consider cyanide-intolerance versus shellfish-intolerance. Both may hold of every human being. Yet only the first is a law. Why? "It is inherent in what it is to be physiologically human (or otherwise mammalian) that a human being be cyanide intolerant." The reason "no one is cyanide-tolerant is that a person's being cyanide-intolerant is rooted in his having a distinctively mammalian physiological make-up."

Shellfish-intolerance, by contrast, is not so rooted. "If everyone happened to be shellfish-intolerant, while otherwise being just like a physiologically normal human being, none of these instances of shellfish-intolerance would be rooted in what it is to have a distinctively human physiology." A deviation would be "a singularity only in some emptily statistical sense."

So the mark of lawhood is this: a law "supports predictions and counterfactuals" whereas an accidental generalization "does not do so." The accidental case—"nothing in any region of space-time that is made of solid gold weighs exactly n oz."—fails precisely because nothing in the nature of gold grounds it.

Laws are grounded in the inherent make-up of things, not in how often they happen to co-occur.

Kuczynski 013 — PASS
Question: Why is causation a relation between events rather than objects?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 11
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 315

EXACT ANSWER
Causation is a relation between events because only events can be causes. Objects are not causes.

This is not a stylistic preference. It is a matter of the logic of the concept. As I have argued, "it is only events that can be causes. Objects are not causes. The rock is not a cause. The rock didn't cause the window to break. What did so was some event involving the rock (e.g., the rock's striking the window at a certain time)."

When we say "Hitler was the cause of World War II," we speak elliptically. Such statements are "elliptical for 'such and such events involving Carter (Hitler) were responsible for the recession in the late '70s (World War II).'" The object drops out; the event remains.

We must also distinguish something's being a cause from its having causal properties. "Non-causes may have causal properties. The rock isn't a cause, as we discussed, but it has causal properties." The rock's mass is a causal property — its having that mass is, or can be, a partial cause. But the rock itself is not the cause.

The same distinction applies to conditions. "We must also distinguish conditions from events. A condition is a relatively persistent state." A room's temperature is a condition; its dropping is an event.

So-called formal and material causes fail for the same reason. "So-called formal causes don't hold between events (or states of affairs) or statements; they hold between things. Thus, the concept of a formal cause is an incoherent one."

Dependence-relations confirm this. "Contrary to what Aristotle thought, dependence-relations don't hold between objects. They hold between events or between statements, depending on whether they're of a causal or a logical nature."

Efficient causality, by contrast, is coherent. "The concept of efficient causality clearly is a coherent one. There is such a thing as efficient causality." And it is "the most fundamental one."

Kuczynski 014 — PASS
Question: Can we observe causal connections?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 9
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 275

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. We observe causal connections constantly—Hume's claim that we don't is not just false but incoherent.

Hume asks, "Do we observe connections between events?" and answers no—"we only observe the events." From this he builds his regularity analysis: E1 causes E2 only if E1 immediately precedes and is adjacent with E2, and any event similar to E1 immediately precedes and is adjacent with some event similar to E2. But this analysis is untenable. It cannot distinguish actual causation from pseudo-causation.

The deeper problem is that Hume's own apparatus presupposes causality. He analyzes causality in terms of spatial occupancy—but "the very notion of spatial occupancy is a causal notion, since a given thing's occupying a given region R ipso facto alters the trajectories and velocities of other would-be and soon-to-be occupants of that region." So "Hume's analysis analyzes causality in terms of itself and therefore doesn't analyze it at all."

Once we see this, the empiricist scruple collapses. "The concept of persistence is itself a causal notion, and anything that persists is, for that very reason, a causal sequence." And since "we see nothing instantaneous"—everything we see persists, if only briefly—"we do see causal processes and, moreover, we see them as causal processes."

Nor is this observation "attenuated" in the way seeing water is seeing H2O molecules. Causal properties are constitutive of the objects themselves: "A 'steel' object that lacked the requisite degree of malleability or thermal conductivity would ipso facto not be steel." Malleability and conductivity are causal properties. To see steel is to see something causally efficacious.

So the answer is unambiguous. We observe causal connections—directly, in the persistence and interaction of ordinary things.

Kuczynski 015 — PASS
Question: Why is persistence itself a causal notion?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 13
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 246

EXACT ANSWER
Persistence is a causal notion because to persist is already to be the continuation of a causal process, not a static fact that could obtain in a world without causation.

I put the point bluntly: "Persistence is a causal phenomenon." And again: "To say that x has endured for a certain period of time, or has moved from A to B, is to make a causal claim." The very concept of something remaining in existence across time carries causal content.

Why? Because "Every case of persistence is a case of continuous change." The chair you sit on persists precisely by continuously changing along every dimension—mass, motion, shape. What we call persistence is a series of states inheriting structure from prior states. That inheritance is causation.

This is why I say "motion and persistence are themselves causal phenomena." And it is why "the persistence of each of those objects is itself a causal fact"—even Y's standing still relative to the pool table is causal, no less than X's rolling.

The consequence is severe for Hume. If persistence is causal, then "no state of affairs can be described by a non-causal proposition, making it impossible even to articulate Hume's puzzle regarding causality in a way that doesn't presuppose its existence."

Hume assumes there are "entirely non-causal descriptions of states of affairs." There are none. Every description of something enduring is already a description of a causal process. His puzzle dissolves.

This topic warrants deeper exploration—consider increasing word count.

Kuczynski 016 — PASS
Question: Why does spatial occupancy imply causal power?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 8
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?
Word count: 284

EXACT ANSWER
To occupy a place is already to exert force. There is no such thing as inert occupancy.

My argument is simple. "To occupy a place is to make it harder, other things equal, than it would otherwise be for other things to enter that place." That is not a contingent fact about matter. It is what occupancy means.

So when Hume treats spatial adjacency as a neutral, pre-causal relation, he has things backwards. "The very notion of spatial occupancy is a causal notion, since a given thing's occupying a given region R ipso facto alters the trajectories and velocities of other would-be and soon-to-be occupants of that region."

Consider what it would mean for this to fail. "It makes no sense to say: 'x is occupying spatial region R, but x's occupancy of R has no effect on what things besides x must do to occupy R.'" The sentence is incoherent, not merely false.

This generalizes. "The concept of persistence is itself a causal notion, and anything that persists is, for that very reason, a causal sequence." And again: "Persistence is a causal phenomenon. Spatio-temporal continuity is a form of persistence. Consequently, continuity is a causal phenomenon."

The consequence for Hume is devastating. "Hume analyzes causality in terms of the concept of spatial occupancy." But if occupancy is itself causal, then "Hume's analysis is viciously circular for this reason." He analyzes causality in terms of itself.

The deeper error is Hume's "falsely 'digital' conception of spatiotemporal reality," in which "each region of space and of time is a distinct box that can be filled with a state of affairs." On that picture, occupancy is a bare label. On mine, it is a causal achievement.

Kuczynski 017 — PASS
Question: What is wrong with Aristotle's notion of formal causes?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 298

EXACT ANSWER
The concept of a formal cause is incoherent. It rests on a category mistake about what dependence-relations attach to.

Aristotle treats forms as causes of things. But "so-called formal causes don't hold between events (or states of affairs) or statements; they hold between things. Thus, the concept of a formal cause is an incoherent one." That is the fatal flaw. Causal and logical dependence-relations hold between events or between statements—not between objects.

Consider what Aristotle means by a formal cause. "x is the formal cause of y if x is y's form." And by "form" he sometimes means shape, sometimes essence—"those properties that are essential to a given thing." But this conflates two entirely different relations.

When I say a triangle must have three sides, I am not describing a causal dependence. I am describing a logical one. "To say that x's having three sides is essential to x's being a triangle is to say that 'x is a triangle' entails that 'x has three sides.'" That is a relation between statements, not between a form and a thing.

The same error infects his essentialism. "Aristotle's belief that things have essences is an incoherent one, and it's a special case of an incoherence that pervades Aristotle's conception of causality." A puddle need not consist of H2O to persist; things undergo chemical changes without ceasing to exist. The dependence is constitutive, not causal.

The deeper point: "dependence-relations don't hold between objects. They hold between events or between statements, depending on whether they're of a causal or a logical nature." Aristotle's formal cause tries to make a logical relation do causal work between things. It cannot.

Only efficient causality survives as fundamental. "The concept of efficient causality clearly is a coherent one. There is such a thing as efficient causality."

[Causality] The Humean denies all this, since he thinks that nothing makes anything happen. But this means that he has trouble accommodating the fact that anything can ever be explained.

[Causality] Suppose two objects to be presented to us, of which the one is the cause and the other the effect; ‘tis plain, that from the simple consideration of one, or both these objects we never shall perceive the tie, by which they are united, or be able certainly to pronounce, that there is a connection between them.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] It is because of its causal properties that T is a tree; it isn't because of its effects on anyone's ideation.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Functionalizing one's awareness of P strips that awareness of causal efficacy, and functionalism is therefore false.
[Causality] But this means that, if functionalism is correct, then the mental does nothing; it has no causal powers at all.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] In identifying causation with correlation, Hume is therefore claiming that to explain an event is to show that it is an accident: to explain is to show that there is no explanation.
[Causality] Because they don’t grant the existence of causality, except of a degraded and useless kind, Humeans are categorically incapable of producing viable theories of confirmation.
[Causality] In fact, the concepts of space and time are to be understood in terms of the concept of causality, and Hume’s analysis is viciously circular for this reason.
[Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence] It follows that properties cannot be identified with sets of their instances without mutilating the most basic principles of logic.
[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] There is a rather serious problem with this view. Consider, not 1 or 2, but rather:
3. Plato snores.
At this point in time, Plato doesn’t exist. So if he’s a (necessary) constituent of 3—if, in other words, 3 exists only if Plato does—then 3 doesn’t exist.
[Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence] Properties lack causal powers. For this reason, they can serve no explanatory function.
[Causality] It thus seems to me that counterfactuals are just disguised causal or logical assertions and that, consequently, alternative worlds have no place in any decent analysis of them.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] It has been said, by inter alia some very great physicists and philosophers, that reality comprises nothing but events, and that "forces" are but "mathematical fictions” with the help of which we state in an abbreviated, tractable form truths that would otherwise be hard to express in a way that served the interests of science (or of ordinary communication).
[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] States of affairs (or “facts,” as they are sometimes called) do not, as Ian Hacking (1975) long ago put it, have the same kinds of “articulations” as propositions.

Kuczynski 018 — PASS
Question: Can goals themselves be causes?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 5
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 279

EXACT ANSWER
No. Goals per se are not causes. They are propositions, and propositions are abstract objects. Abstract objects do no causal work.

This is the crux of my disagreement with Aristotle on final causes. If final causes are goals per se, he is wrong to call them causes. As I put it: "Goals per se do nothing. Goals are abstract objects and therefore do no causal work."

What does causal work is a person's having a goal. My having the goal that I get in shape causes me to jog around the block for hours on end. But the goal itself—the proposition—does nothing. "My having that goal is my intending to make that proposition come true. But my having a goal is a garden-variety efficient cause."

So Aristotle faces a dilemma. "In saying there are such things as 'final' causes, Aristotle is either making the trivial point that a person's having a goal can have effects or he is making the deeply confused point that goals per se can have effects."

The same logic applies to objects generally. A rock weighs three pounds, and "it is in virtue of this fact about the rock that certain events involving the rock have certain effects." But "the rock per se is not a cause." Objects possess causal properties; they are not themselves causes.

The lesson is uniform: "In general, goals per se, being propositions, aren't causes at all. A given person's (or creature's) having a given goal is a cause. But it's a garden-variety efficient cause."

Efficient causality is the fundamental form. "There is such a thing as efficient causality... it's the most fundamental one." Final causes, properly understood, collapse into it.

[Causality] Causal claims are themselves explanations. In being told that lung cancer is what led to Smith’s death, I’m being given an explanation, albeit an incomplete one, as to why he died.

Kuczynski 019 — PASS
Question: Why does Hume's analysis fail to distinguish causation from epiphenomenal succession?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 10
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 273

EXACT ANSWER
Hume's analysis fails because it cannot distinguish a genuine causal connection from a mere sequence of effects that happen to share a common cause. His conditions are satisfied by both.

Take my example of Fido. "Your dog Fido is an infallible earthquake detector." When an earthquake is imminent, he barks and runs in circles. The earthquake then strikes. Fido's antics immediately precede the earthquake, are adjacent with it, and antics of that kind are always adjacent to an ensuing earthquake. So all three of Hume's conditions are met.

Yet "Hume's analysis entails that Fido's antics causes the earthquake to strike." That is absurd. The real cause is the incipient tectonic disturbance, which produced both the dog's behavior and the quake. As I put it, "some one thing was responsible both for Fido's antics and for the earthquake. The two effects fulfilled the conditions described by Hume."

This is the general defect. "When some one thing E1 has two effects E2 and E3 that appear to be related as cause and effect, respectively, we say that the sequence consisting of E2 and E3 is a 'pseudo-causal process' or, alternately, a case of 'epiphenomenal causation.'" Hume's machinery cannot tell this apart from real causation.

The same point appears with day and night: "the rotation of the Earth with respect to Sun causes day to follow (and be adjacent with) night, etc. But day doesn't cause night." Reid saw this too.

What Hume misses is the mechanism—the continuity. "It's the mechanism, the continuity, that is the sine qua non of causality." Constant conjunctions merely tip us off to continuities; they are not themselves the causal tie.

[Causality] Aristotle’s belief that things have essences is an incoherent one, and it’s a special case of an incoherence that pervades Aristotle’s conception of causality.

Kuczynski 020 — PASS
Question: What is the relation between causation and explanation?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 5
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 255

EXACT ANSWER
To explain an event is to identify its cause. That is the platitude I begin with: "To explain is to find causes; to explain an event is to identify its cause."

But this platitude conceals the real work. Hume and Hempel reverse the order of understanding. They hold that "the event is to be explained in terms of the law; they believe that laws are logically prior to events." I deny this. "It's the other way around."

People already possess explanations—low-level, low-resolution ones—of the phenomena they encounter. Only on that basis do they posit laws. "Hypotheses concerning laws are distillations of existing, low-level explanations."

This matters because it exposes the circularity at the heart of the deductive-nomological model. "DN is viciously circular and is therefore a failure."

What, then, drives the search for explanation? One principle: "causal anomalies must be minimized." And how are they minimized? "The elimination of causal anomalies consists in the elimination of discontinuities."

So the relation is this: explanation is the identification of causes, and causation is a relation between events—not things. "Causation is a relation between events, not between objects or things."

Hume's error was to identify causation with correlation. The consequence is devastating: "to explain an event is to show that it is an accident: to explain is to show that there is no explanation." That is "the methodological significance of empiricism."

Explanation and causation are not two separate matters. Explanation just is the articulation of causal structure—and where empiricism severs cause from mechanism, it severs explanation from itself.

[Causality] Contrary to what Aristotle thought, dependence-relations don’t hold between objects. They hold between events or between statements, depending on whether they’re of a causal or a logical nature.
[Causality] Events have causal roots. Two worlds cannot differ with respect to a single event unless they differ with respect to all of the causal antecedents of those events.

[Causality] The concept of probability is to be understood in terms of the concept of explanatory integrity, not that of statistical frequency.

Kuczynski 021 — PASS
Question: Do people think in natural language?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 8
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Do We Think in Words?; Part II; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 291

EXACT ANSWER
No. We do not think in natural language, and we do not think in any innate code either.

The thesis comes in two versions. Version #1 holds that "English speakers think in English; Albanian speakers think in Albanian; etc." Version #2 holds that we think in some innate, universal code. I reject both.

Against Version #1: thoughts are not identical with sentence-tokens. My thinking that snow is cold is not the same as the sentence "snow is cold" flashing through my mind. As I put it, "if your thinking if snow is cold, then snow is not hot were identical with your thinking 'if snow is cold, then snow is not hot,' then your having that thought would be identical with that sentence's flashing through your mind. Since it isn't, we don't think in natural language."

The same point applies across languages. "People who speak different languages can have the same thoughts." A monolingual Spanish speaker and a monolingual English speaker both believe 1 + 1 = 2, yet they use different sentences. So the thought cannot be the sentence.

Against Version #2: we often think in images, and "the contents of these images seem to have a structure that is fundamentally different from the structure of any sentence." Sentences are digital; sense-perceptions are analogue. Moreover, "knowing a language involves understanding the expressions belonging to it," and one cannot understand any language, innate or not, without knowing its rules.

Why does it feel as though we think in words? Because language enhances cognition enormously. When I'm working, "my thoughts often take the form of a voice." But the voice is a representation, not the thought itself.

We don't think in language. Language is a powerful tool for thought, not its medium.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS
[Do We Think in Words?] People who speak different languages can have the same thoughts. Monolingual Spanish speakers believe that 1 + 1 = 2, and so do monolingual English speakers. But English and Spanish speakers use different sentences to express that truth, showing that thinking that truth cannot be identical with thinking a sentence of English or Spanish or, by obvious extensions of this line of thought, any other language.
[Do We Think in Words?] We can obviously have thoughts that we are incapable of putting into words. This shows that we grasp those thoughts in some way other than by grasping the English sentences that express them. Also, there are occasions where, although we can put into words what we are thinking, doing so requires great effort: we have to think long and hard about which words to use. This shows that thinking is often a prerequisite to using language, which in turn shows that at least some thinking is non-linguistic.
[Do We Think in Words?] But untutored intuitions are not always probative. And sometimes they are inconsistent with one another, this being one of those cases. For, while we are reluctant to impute discursive thought to the language-less, we also strongly feel that we sometimes have thoughts of considerable sophistication that we don’t know how to put into words; and, if that’s right (as I personally have no doubt that it is), then it seems to follow that not all discursive thought is language-dependent.
[Do We Think in Words?] Wittgenstein’s view that thinking consists of manipulating expressions is inconsistent with the fact that, unless one can think, one cannot use expressions meaningfully. If I just bark out sounds, having no idea what they mean, I’m not in any real sense using language. For my noise-making to be speech, it must be guided by a knowledge of what those noises mean.
[Do We Think in Words?] Some kinds of information (e.g., visual information) seem inherently incapable of being fully expressed by sentences of English or of any other natural language. Also, sentences have very different structures from pictures, even when the sentences and the pictures overlap in content.
[Do We Think in Words?] By virtue of knowing a language, one automatically has at one’s disposal a rich and easily operated system of icons by means of which one can manage one’s cognitive activities in the way just described. This, I would suggest, is why knowledge of a language does so much for thought.
[Do We Think in Words?] Thus, there are languages only where there are right and wrong ways to use expressions. Since it is only where several people are using a language that there are right and wrong ways of using its expressions, it is only where there are several people that there are languages: all languages are public.
[Part II] In natural language, quantified sentences are pseudo-objectual statements. They appear to be about objects, but are really about properties.
[Do We Think in Words?] A language is a set of semantic rules. If, on Monday, “X35” means that the berries on the dark side of the hill are poisonous, whereas on Tuesday that same inscription means that the sun is out, then the language Smith is using on Tuesday is ipso facto a distinct one from the language he is using on Monday.
[Do We Think in Words?] In order to learn a language, one must already be able to think. To learn English is to learn that certain sounds have certain meanings; and in order to be able to do this, you already have to be able to synthesize information—to make judgments, on the basis of experience, as to what sounds have what meanings. So knowledge of a language presupposes the ability to think.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Custom-driven behavior is not rule-governed behavior. The former therefore isn't true or false, or otherwise right or wrong, and it therefore isn't linguistic.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] In order for anyone to say anything, words must have meanings, and there must therefore be true propositions saying what those meanings are.
[Do We Think in Words?] Thinking seems to involve following rules. Wittgenstein grants that people think. But since he believes totally absurdly, that rule-following and, therefore, thinking aren’t psychological acts, he is forced to come up
with a different analysis of what thought is.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Operating independently of each other, Wittgenstein and Quine, both staunch empiricists, came to the same, radically counterintuitive conclusion, to wit: (QW113) When trying to figure out what another person means, one must focus only on his body movements.
[Do We Think in Words?] The expressions of Smithese1, then, don’t mean whatever Smith thinks they mean. If his opinions assign new meanings to X1...Xn, those opinions are creating a new language that happens to be orthographically and phonetically coincident with Smithese1; those opinions aren’t assigning new meanings to expressions constituting some existing language.
[Part II] Nothing meaningless is an expression. For this reason, the concept of an expression must be understood in terms of the concept of meaning, the same therefore being true of the concept of language.
[Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6] The problem is that Frege’s theory doesn’t entirely coincide with Russell’s. Unlike Russell, Frege took it for granted that definite descriptions are just what, given their grammar, they appear to be: referring terms.
[Part II] Changes in what we believe affect what we infer from statements; but they don’t categorically change the meanings of those statements.
[Part II] Where complex expressions are concerned, there is no limit to how much literal and understood meaning may diverge from each other. But where simple expressions are concerned, literal and understood meaning must coalesce.
[Part II] Some expressions appear to have a two-tiered semantic structure. For example, an occurrence of the pronoun “I” has a referent, this being the person who uttered it, and it picks out that referent in a certain way (i.e., by way of a certain concept).

Kuczynski 022 — PASS
Question: Why must thought be possible before language can be learned?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 11
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Do We Think in Words?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 275

EXACT ANSWER
Thought must be possible before language can be learned because learning a language is itself a cognitive achievement—one that presupposes the very capacity it supposedly produces. To learn English is to learn that certain sounds have certain meanings, and that requires the ability to synthesize information before any of those sounds mean anything to you.

My third reason for rejecting the "thinking in language" view is precisely this: "In order to learn a language, one must already be able to think. To learn English is to learn that certain sounds have certain meanings; and in order to be able to do this, you already have to be able to synthesize information—to make judgments, on the basis of experience, as to what sounds have what meanings."

So "knowledge of a language presupposes the ability to think." The dependency runs one way. You cannot acquire the meanings of words unless you can already form judgments about which noises go with which things.

The same point appears in my sixth reason. "Understanding sentences involves thought." To understand "if snow is cold, then snow is not hot," you must pair the sentence with the right meaning—and that pairing is not itself a sentence flashing through your mind. A sentence I don't understand can still echo in my head; I remember the sounds but grasp nothing.

And this is fatal to the Wittgensteinian line. "Wittgenstein's view that thinking consists of manipulating expressions is inconsistent with the fact that, unless one can think, one cannot use expressions meaningfully." Barking sounds without knowing what they mean is not speech.

Thinking is the precondition. Language is the artifact built on top of it.

This shows that thinking is often a prerequisite to using language, which in turn shows that at least some thinking is non-linguistic.

[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Just as Searle believes, cognitive processes are realized by physiological processes. But it doesn't follow, and it's a priori improbable, that the psychological relations between physiological events can be understood in physiological terms.
[Part II] Nothing meaningless is an expression. For this reason, the concept of an expression must be understood in terms of the concept of meaning, the same therefore being true of the concept of language.
[Do We Think in Words?] Given this data, I would suggest the following. Though not themselves constitutive of thought, mental images allow us to manage thought-processes that we otherwise couldn’t manage. Just as one can manipulate otherwise uncontrollable patterns of electrical activity by manipulating the icons on one’s computer desktop, so one can manipulate otherwise uncontrollable patterns of ratiocinative activity by manipulating cognitive imagery.
[Do We Think in Words?] But even if I were more visually adept and, for that reason, could produce more exact visual surrogates for the concepts I’m thinking about, my ratiocinative activity couldn’t possibly be identical with any sequence of images. For any given sequence of images, a creature that could experience those images wouldn’t necessarily have any of the concepts that they represent in my thought processes.
[Do We Think in Words?] A language is a set of semantic rules. If, on Monday, “X35” means that the berries on the dark side of the hill are poisonous, whereas on Tuesday that same inscription means that the sun is out, then the language Smith is using on Tuesday is ipso facto a distinct one from the language he is using on Monday.
[Do We Think in Words?] Thus, there are languages only where there are right and wrong ways to use expressions. Since it is only where several people are using a language that there are right and wrong ways of using its expressions, it is only where there are several people that there are languages: all languages are public.
[Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism] Sellars has shown that in non-standard conditions, one must rely (however unconsciously) on background knowledge if one is to learn, through one’s senses, how things are in the external world.
[Do We Think in Words?] Wittgenstein’s position is rank absurdity. He’s right, of course, that tickles, itches, etc., aren’t thoughts. But what follows isn’t that thoughts aren’t mental entities. What follows is that thoughts, unlike itches and tickles, aren’t phenomenologically pregnant.
[Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible] The mind is not a perfectly unified entity; it consists of various parts that communicate with each other only very imperfectly. The right hand doesn’t always know what the left is doing.
[Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible] So, speaking epistemologically, these psychological “intangibles” are much more tangible, and their existence infinitely less dubitable, than “tangible” things such as brains.

Kuczynski 023 — PASS
Question: Can a person have a sophisticated thought that cannot yet be put into words?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 11
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 291

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. This is not a marginal case—it is the norm, and it demolishes the thesis that we think in language.

My argument is straightforward. "We can obviously have thoughts that we are incapable of putting into words. This shows that we grasp those thoughts in some way other than by grasping the English sentences that express them." And more than that: "there are occasions where, although we can put into words what we are thinking, doing so requires great effort: we have to think long and hard about which words to use. This shows that thinking is often a prerequisite to using language, which in turn shows that at least some thinking is non-linguistic."

The counterintuitive part is that this holds even for sophisticated, discursive thought. I grant that "we are reluctant to regard creatures that cannot interact with reality through a language... as being capable of grasping, let alone delineating the consequences of, abstract principles." But this reluctance is not probative. For "while we are reluctant to impute discursive thought to the language-less, we also strongly feel that we sometimes have thoughts of considerable sophistication that we don't know how to put into words."

Consider the empiricist objection. Authors like Wittgenstein, Blackburn, and McDowell insist that "if you can't say it, you don't know it." This "presupposes that what we believe is expressed only in the words we utter. But that's not the case." A person's knowledge "may be expressed in that person's drawings of x and y; or it may be expressed in that person's movements while he is in the vicinity of x and y; or it may be expressed in some other non-linguistic manner."

So the sophisticated thought is there, fully formed, awaiting articulation—not created by it.

Kuczynski 024 — PASS
Question: What is wrong with the language-of-thought hypothesis?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Do We Think in Words?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Causality
Word count: 279

EXACT ANSWER
The language-of-thought hypothesis fails because it cannot account for the fact that we think in something other than sentences. My first reason: if thoughts were sentences, then understanding a sentence would require understanding another sentence, and so on forever. But "it is viciously circular to hold that we think in an innately known code."

My second reason: people who speak different languages have the same thoughts. "Monolingual Spanish speakers believe that 1 + 1 = 2, and so do monolingual English speakers." But they use different sentences. So thinking a truth cannot be identical with thinking a sentence of any particular language.

My third reason: understanding sentences involves thought, not the other way around. "Understanding a sentence is different from its flashing through your mind." A sentence of a language I don't understand can pass through my head without my knowing what it means.

My fourth reason: much knowledge is non-linguistic. "A person's knowledge of the differences between x and y may be expressed in that person's drawings of x and y; or it may be expressed in that person's movements." Wittgenstein, Blackburn, and McDowell deny this, but "this is obviously false."

The advocates of an innate code try to escape by saying we don't understand the sentences of that code—we are merely "built to conform" to them. But "if a sentence isn't understood by so and so, then from so and so's viewpoint it is not functioning as a sentence." A book used to hit your roommate is not functioning as a book. So we may conclude "that we don't think in language, even though, undeniably, knowledge of language enormously enhances at least some of our cognitive capabilities."

Kuczynski 025 — PASS
Question: How does Russell's theory of descriptions improve on Frege's semantics?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 8
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Do We Think in Words?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Causality
Word count: 257

EXACT ANSWER
Russell's theory of descriptions—what I call TD—solves the problems Frege's system cannot. The core improvement is that definite descriptions are quantifiers, not referring terms.

Frege treats "the inventor of bifocals" as a referring expression. This generates catastrophe. As I argue, "a consequence of Frege's system is that no two of the occurrences of 'snow' in (D) 'snow is white, but Brad thinks that snow is green...' co-refer, and only one of those occurrences (the first one) refers to snow." That is absurd.

The hard data is simple. Consider Smith and the inventor of bifocals, who is also the first postmaster general. Frege's theory predicts that substituting co-referring terms preserves truth-value. It doesn't. "1 may be false if 3 is true and vice versa."

TD dissolves the problem. On my analysis, "Smith believes that something uniquely invented bifocals, and any such thing is 6 ft tall." The description is not a singular term at all—it is a quantifier. So "1R doesn't entail 3R, and 1R is compatible with 4R."

Frege saw this and patched it with intensional contexts. "Frege was aware of the problem just described, and he added an epicycle to his theory that he hoped would save it. It did not."

The deeper point: "the word 'the' obviously indicates existence (unlike the word 'no') and also uniqueness (unlike the word 'a')." TD captures this. "So (#) is true exactly if: (##) Exactly one thing x is a current U.S. President, and x smokes."

Frege's position is, as I've said, "logically and methodologically indefensible." TD is not.

Kuczynski 026 — PASS
Question: Why is a definite description not a singular term?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 14
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Do We Think in Words?; Part II; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 327

EXACT ANSWER
A definite description is not a singular term because it does not refer to anything at all. "Definite descriptions aren't referring terms. No definite description refers to anything." The meaning of "the inventor of Velcro" is not given by saying who it refers to, but by saying what whole sentences containing it mean.

This is the core of the Theory of Descriptions. As I put it, the essence of the theory is given by two assertions: "(i) definite descriptions don't refer to anything; and (ii) in virtue of containing a definite description, a sentence attributes properties to other properties, not to individuals."

Consider what happens when we embed a description in a sentence. "The inventor of Velcro is seven-feet tall" doesn't attribute the property of being 7-feet tall to anyone. Instead, "that sentence says that the property of being a unique inventor of Velcro is instantiated and, in addition, that the property of being a 7-feet tall Velcro-inventor is also instantiated." The sentence is about properties, not individuals.

The contrast with genuine singular terms is decisive. Take a proper name like "Bob" and a description like "the inventor of Velcro." Even if Bob invented Velcro, the two expressions say different things. "V would be false in a situation where Bob was intelligent and where some unintelligent person had invented Velcro. But B would be true in such a situation." Different truth-conditions mean different semantics.

So descriptions function as quantifiers, not singular terms. "The inventor of bifocals" seems to refer to Benjamin Franklin. But "the inventor of bifocals snored" is true iff exactly one thing invented bifocals, and any such snored. What this shows is that, in virtue of containing an occurrence of "the inventor of bifocals," an expression encodes an existence-claim.

That is why the grammatical form of such sentences is not to be trusted. Definite descriptions are to be defined contextually, and sentences containing them must be reparsed if their meaning is to be made clear.

Kuczynski 027 — PASS
Question: What does the sentence 'The present King of France is bald' logically assert?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 226

EXACT ANSWER
The sentence asserts a quantified generalization, not a subject-predicate attribution to a nonexistent object. According to my Theory of Descriptions, "the king of France is bald" has for its meaning a quantified generalization that is either identical or equivalent with: something x is uniquely a king of France; moreover, x is bald.

So the logical form is not B(k), where k names the present King of France. There is no such k. Instead we get an existential claim with three conjuncts: there exists an x such that x is a king of France, x is unique in being so, and x is bald.

This is why the sentence is meaningful but false rather than meaningless. Its falsity is not a failure of reference; it is the failure of an existential condition. The generalization asserts that something satisfies a complex predicate, and nothing does.

The apparent subject "the present King of France" is not a logically proper name at all. It is a disguised description, and descriptions are not referring expressions but quantified phrases. Treating them as names generates the false puzzle of how we can think about nonexistent objects.

My conclusion stands: "'The king of France is bald' means a quantified generalization according to Russell's Theory of Descriptions." The surface grammar misleads; the logical grammar reveals a claim about what exists and how it is characterized.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS

[Part II] Thus, what a sentence literally means is only one of many factors governing what it is used to mean.
[Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6] The going line before Russell was to say that it says of some object that it doesn’t exist. But that makes no sense at all, since it’s the same as saying “there exists an object (viz. a certain monster) that doesn’t exist.”

3. Plato snores.
At this point in time, Plato doesn’t exist. So if he’s a (necessary) constituent of 3—if, in other words, 3 exists only if Plato does—then 3 doesn’t exist.

Kuczynski 028 — PASS
Question: Why can grammatical form conceal logical form?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Part II; Do We Think in Words?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Causality
Word count: 275

EXACT ANSWER
Grammatical form conceals logical form because the two can genuinely pull apart—and they pull apart precisely when a sentence contains a quantifier. As I put it, "Given any statement of English, or any other non-artificial language, that contains quantifiers, the logical and grammatical forms of that sentence diverge."

Take "whales are mammals." Its grammar makes it look as though it names whales and says something about them. It doesn't. "WM makes a statement, not about whales, but about the property of being a whale." The surface grammar points at objects; the logical form points at a property.

The mechanism is the quantifier. "A quantifier is an expression having the property that, if a sentence contains it, that sentence is ipso facto to the effect that the extension of one property has a certain degree of overlap... with the extension of some other property." So "All birds have beaks" says that "the extension of the property of being a bird is a subset of the extension of the property of having a beak."

Here is the crux: "it is in virtue of containing a quantifier that a sentence concerns properties." Grammar hides this because it presents quantified statements as if they were about individuals.

"Nothing snores" is the cleanest case. Its grammar "seems to license the inference that there exists some strange entity—the nothing, or some such—that snores." But that inference is bogus. The logical form is "the property of snoring is un-instantiated."

So grammatical form conceals logical form because grammar is not built to display quantificational structure. A perspicuous paraphrase—one that "makes it clear what S entails and what entails S"—reveals what the grammar obscures.

Kuczynski 029 — PASS
Question: What is a desire?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 279

EXACT ANSWER
A desire is a belief. Specifically, it is a judgment to the effect that some state of affairs would be a source of gratification.

I want to eat that piece of cake. If I believed that cake to be made of chalk-dust or mud, I wouldn't want to eat it. The reason: I'd believe that eating it would not be a source of gratification. By the same token, since I believe that eating that piece of cake would be exquisitely pleasurable, I do want to eat it. My desire to eat that piece of cake, I therefore submit, is identical with a judgment on my part: a judgment to the effect that eating it would be pleasurable.

The same analysis applies to aversion. I don't currently want to drink the contents of that bottle of dishwashing detergent. The reason: I believe that it would bring me no joy and much distress. My desire to abstain from consuming the contents of that bottle is a judgment: a judgment to the effect that failing to so abstain would bring me much grief.

This has a crucial consequence. One doesn't have to have a belief to be in an unpleasant state. But one does have to have a belief if one is to have a desire to alleviate or otherwise respond to that state. One doesn't have a desire to take a pill unless one has a belief as to what taking that pill will do. And if one has such a belief, one has the corresponding desire.

Desires are not brute pushes. They are propositional attitudes. A desire is a belief as to how to achieve gratification of a certain kind.
