Kuczynski 030 — PASS
Question: Why is an intention not identical with one's strongest desire?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 9
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Do We Think in Words?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 285

EXACT ANSWER
An intention is a judgment, not a desire. That is the whole of it.

The empiricist conflates them. Davidson holds that "what one intends to do in a given context C is identical with what one most wants to do in C." He concludes that "one's C-intention is one's strongest C-desire." This is false.

Take the robbery. A man puts a gun in my face. I intend to hand over my money. Do I want to? No. "I don't want to give the gunman my money." I want to live, but "that doesn't mean I want to do all the things that are involved in staying alive." I judge that giving him the money is the lesser evil. My intention is "a judgment that I'll be better off if I give the gunman my money than if I don't."

Contrast this with genuine ambivalence. If I love my mother but she has written a negative review of my book, "I also want to not give her a birthday present." There I have two competing desires. In the robbery, I have no desire to comply at all. "I see giving him my money as a grim concession I must make to circumstances that I wholeheartedly wish I didn't have to make."

The empiricist's error runs deeper. He treats all motivated behavior as desire-driven. But much of it is judgment-driven. Consider Frankfurt's higher-order desires. "Many a so-called HOD is really a judgment." When I exercise self-control to pass a fireman's exam, "my behavior isn't desire-driven in any sense; it's judgement-driven."

So the identity thesis fails. An intention can exist without any corresponding desire. It is a practical judgment about what is best to do, all things considered.

Kuczynski 031 — PASS
Question: Can a desire remain present when it is outweighed by another desire?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Causality; Do We Think in Words?
Word count: 311

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. A desire does not cease to exist merely because it is outweighed.

I make this point directly in the context of coercion. When a gunman demands my money, I judge that "if I am to gratify a relatively strong desire (my desire to live) I must forego gratification of a relatively weak one (my desire to hold on to my money)." But I insist that "this is very different from wanting to give the man my money."

The weaker desire persists. As I put it, "Desires don't cease to exist when they are outweighed." My desire for money does not vanish because my desire to live is stronger. "If somebody showed up with a briefcase full of money, I couldn't be more delighted." And in the robbery itself, "if a stroke of good fortune were to intervene (if, for example, the gunman were to drop dead of a heart-attack), I couldn't be happier."

That reaction is the proof. "If, while being robbed, I didn't want to not give the man my money, I wouldn't respond this way."

The objection is that ambivalence is possible — that "one can want both to do and to refrain from X." I grant the logical point: "What's not possible is for it to be the case both that desires, and has no desire, to do X." But that is not what is happening in the robbery case. There, I genuinely want to keep my money; I simply want to live more.

This is the same structure I find in moral obligation. An obligation can be "merely outweighed" without ceasing to exist. The obligation not to steal "did hold categorically" even when saving my children required stealing. The remorse I feel is not "the emotional counterpart of a hallucination" — it tracks a real, surviving obligation.

Desires work the same way. Outweighing is not elimination.

Kuczynski 032 — PASS
Question: How are values related to agency?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Causality; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 253

EXACT ANSWER
Values are constitutive of agency, not merely instrumental to it. To be an agent is to be governed by values.

My framework is this: "To value something, I propose, is to see it as conducive to the consolidation or enlargement of one's agency." The ur-value—"the value underlying all values"—is "the extension and consolidation of agency." Everything else we value derives its worth from this root.

This has a sharp consequence. "It is only to the extent that one's behavior is guided by superstructure-specific desires that one is acting, rather than reflexively reacting. Therefore, it is only to that extent that one is an agent." Strip away value-drivenness and you strip away agency itself.

The mechanism is normative cohesion. "Value-drivenness is the glue that makes a multiplicity of otherwise disconnected mental states constitute a single subject." Without it, you have mental states associated with a body, but no mind—no self.

So the relationship runs both directions. Values fortify agency, and agency is what makes values possible in the first place. "Human beings have values. This is what distinguishes us from other sentient beings."

Even morality has deeper roots than we admit. "A sensitivity to value, and thus a kind of morality, lies at the root of all subjecthood." The psychopath, who "can't value anyone or anything," fails at the level of agency itself—not merely at the level of ethics.

This is why self-remodeling that voids one's values is ruinous. Sally's pre-Tim values "were the nucleus of her agency." Destroy them and you destroy her.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] But there is a norm that is much more fundamental than any socio-ethical norm. That norm is given by the proposition that what we should value is the extension and consolidation of agency.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] To value something, I propose, is to see it as conducive to the consolidation or enlargement of one's agency.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8] The criminal is meaningfully connected to some people; and the ability to have such a connection presupposes the ability to have values; and it thus presupposes that one values the truth, since one must value the truth to value anything. So even if the criminal is meaningfully connected to but one other human being, his being able to have that connection means that he has values and thus has the psychological architecture needed to mediate value-driven ideation. This, in turn, means that he is an agent.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Whether Sally's pre-Tim values were good or bad is irrelevant. What is relevant is that they were her values. They were the nucleus of her agency.

Kuczynski 033 — PASS
Question: Why must an agent value truth in order to value anything?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Causality
Word count: 297

EXACT ANSWER
To value X is to believe it a truth that X has some virtue or other. That is the whole argument in one sentence.

If one doesn't value the truth, one can't see X as inherently better than non-X. So the capacity to value anything at all presupposes the capacity to value truth. Truth is not one value among others—it is the precondition of valuation as such.

This is why I say that the ability to have such a connection presupposes the ability to have values; and it thus presupposes that one values the truth, since one must value the truth to value anything.

Consider what happens when this architecture breaks down. The person who retreats into fantasy has lost the distinction between fact and fiction. He sees them as interchangeable. This means he no longer has the concept of truth and therefore cannot value the truth. This means that he values nothing.

The psychopath illustrates the same point from the other direction. The fact that the psychopath can't value anyone or anything means he lacks the psychological architecture needed to mediate value-driven ideation. Without truth-valuation, there is no valuing at all.

This is why I insist that only agents can be evil and only agents can be criminals. Agency requires values; values require truth-valuation; and truth-valuation requires the concept of truth. Strip that away and you strip away the agent himself.

Even the criminal retains this architecture so long as he is meaningfully connected to but one other human being. His being able to have that connection means that he has values and thus has the psychological architecture needed to mediate value-driven ideation. This, in turn, means that he is an agent.

Truth is the load-bearing wall. Remove it and the whole structure of valuation collapses.

Kuczynski 034 — PASS
Question: What distinguishes evil from hatred?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?
Word count: 318

EXACT ANSWER
Evil is not merely intense hatred. It is hatred that has lost its object and hardened into a permanent posture toward being itself.

"Evil must be distinguished from hate. Everyone hates someone, but not everyone is evil." That is the first cut. Hatred is universal and particular. Evil is neither.

The distinction is structural, not quantitative. "Evil is generalized, rigidified hate." Two features do the work: generalization and rigidity. Your hatred has a target — Jerry, say — and when Jerry is gone, the hatred can dissolve. Evil has no such exit. "Whereas your hatred is person-specific, its target being Jerry, Stalin's was not, its target being humanity itself."

So the difference is this: hatred is a relation to a particular object; evil is a relation to the category of persons as such. The evil agent does not hate this or that human being. He hates the human.

There is also a modal difference. Hatred can be episodic, reactive, even justified. Evil is a standing disposition — rigidified — that no longer answers to reasons or to changes in its objects. It has become a fixed feature of the agent's psychology.

This matters for how we theorize. "Psychological theory must make some provisions for the phenomenon of evil separate from those that it makes or doesn't make, but should - for criminality, sociopathy, and psychopathy." Evil is not reducible to criminality or to the psychopath's deficit. "Wickedness and criminality are not mutually exclusive: only agents can be evil and only agents can be criminals, and a given agent can be both."

The psychopath is a different case entirely. "The fact that the psychopath can't value anyone or anything" means he lacks the architecture for evil. Evil requires an agent — one who can value, and therefore one who can hate in the rigidified, generalized way that constitutes evil.

Hatred is a passion. Evil is a deformation of agency.

Kuczynski 035 — PASS
Question: What makes unconscious mental activity necessary for explaining conscious thought?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 326

EXACT ANSWER
Conscious thought is a surface phenomenon. It is the visible tip of a process whose bulk lies beneath. To explain what happens at the surface, you must posit what happens below it.

Freud saw this first. He "proposed that conscious psychological episodes are installments in sequences of events large portions of which are constituted by unconscious psychological activity." If that is right, then "psychological reality is continuous and law-governed, notwithstanding the erratic and discontinuous nature of activity within the sphere of consciousness."

The empiricist cannot accept this. But the empiricist also cannot explain anything. "There is no way to understand the concept of repression in strictly empiricist terms, the same being true of the concept of rationalization." These are not empirical discoveries. They are "definitional truths." "Repressed ideation is by definition unconscious, and rationalizations by definition involve a bifurcation of mental activity into conscious and unconscious strata."

So the necessity is structural, not optional. "The hypothesis that there is unconscious mental activity is the ultimate deviation from the strictures of empiricism. But there is no way to explain conscious mental activity except on the assumption that there is unconscious mental activity."

Consider the ordinary case. "Most data-synthesis occurs below the threshold of consciousness." When you enter a room, you "automatically upload a mass of information" and "automatically make many inferences from that data." None of this requires your conscious cooperation. "If it did, you'd know very little indeed."

Even your standing intentions prove the point. My intention to build a trading platform "was not the result of the contents of my sense-perceptions in some specific situation; it developed on the basis of judgments made in response to a wide range of situations and linked together by protracted and therefore largely unconscious thought-processes." A standing intention "demonstrably cannot possibly be a constituent of consciousness."

Hence the sterility of empiricist psychology. The unconscious is not a metaphysical indulgence. It is the only thing that makes conscious life intelligible.

Kuczynski 036 — PASS
Question: How is repression possible?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 13
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 251

EXACT ANSWER
Repression is possible because of three facts about the mind's architecture.

First, "the mind is not a perfectly unified entity; it consists of various parts that communicate with each other only very imperfectly." The right hand doesn't always know what the left is doing.

Second, there are two ways of being aware of a thing—knowledge by acquaintance and knowledge by description—and these can come apart.

Third, "there are two very different senses in which a given mental content can be 'conscious.'" A content can be a constituent of consciousness, or it can be something of which one is conscious.

Given this architecture, repression1 becomes intelligible. It is "a failure to do something; it is a failure to perform the operations by which one customarily generates one's beliefs; it isn't an operation performed on existing beliefs." When we speak of "repressed knowledge," we refer "to suspicions that we have chosen not to verify, since we suspect that, were we to do so, we'd be stuck with knowledge that we wish we didn't have."

The conventional picture is wrong. It says the patient "knowingly chooses to cease to be aware of" some attitude and that this attitude is "constantly struggling to break into consciousness." That is paradoxical—to keep something out, one would have to know what it is, in which case it's already unrepressed.

The real conflict is not between unconscious knowledge and conscious ignorance. "The conflict is between a wish to know the truth and a wish to not know the truth."

Kuczynski 037 — PASS
Question: What is the difference between having a belief and knowing that one has it?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Part II; Causality
Word count: 252

EXACT ANSWER
Having a belief and knowing that one has it are two distinct things. One can feel a certain way towards so and so but not know it.

The distinction is this: "one must distinguish having a given propositional attitude (or, more generally, mental content) from knowing that one has it." Knowing that one feels a certain way is not the same as feeling it. It "involves making a judgment about one's mental state."

This matters because propositional attitudes are never directly present to consciousness. "Propositional attitudes never make an appearance in consciousness in propria persona---or, rather, they do so, but only in the indirect sense in which rocks and trees do." What is in consciousness is "at best some representative or derivative of a propositional attitude."

So when Smith feels a surge of anger towards his dad, what he has is the attitude. What he has in consciousness is a derivative. Whether he knows he has the attitude requires a further judgment—one he may or may not make.

This is why self-deception is possible. "Self-deception is not mere ignorance of what one is feeling." It "seems to presuppose that, at some level, one has knowledge of the thing of which one lacks conscious knowledge." The gap between having and knowing opens the space for that internal conflict—"between what one knows and what one wants to know, between what is unconscious (because repressed) and what is conscious (because countenanced)."

Without this gap, one would be "completely transparent to oneself." But we are not.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS

One can choose to do things that one knows will add mass to one’s body; one cannot choose to gain weight in the way in which one can choose to move one’s arm.)

Kuczynski 038 — PASS
Question: Why can rationalization preserve a belief while concealing its real basis?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Causality
Word count: 270

EXACT ANSWER
Rationalization preserves a belief by severing it from everything that would expose it. The mechanism is decontextualization. As I put it, "the essence of rationalization is decontextualization. The very purpose of rationalizing is to isolate bodies of information that, if allowed to commingle, will lead to an awareness of the very thing one doesn't want to know."

This is why the rationalizer must view things "as narrowly as possible." If he doesn't, "he'll be either be forced to draw unwelcome conclusions from the data that he already has or he'll put himself in situations where the data is rationalization-proof."

The belief survives because its real basis is never allowed into the same room as its stated justification. Each explanation, taken alone, is "modestly credible." But "none has much credibility when taken in the context of Sally's other explanations."

So rationalizing is not lying. "To lie is to intentionally misrepresent the truth to another person. To rationalize is to suppress one's own knowledge of some truth and to choose to replace it with a belief that one knows to be false." And crucially, "rationalizing involves two things: (i) Lying to oneself; (ii) Inventing a story to make the lie believable." Hence the rule: "No fraud without a fairy tale."

The cover-up compounds. "Each instance of repression2 creates a hole in one's body of knowledge that one must explain away." Since these explanations "cannot be genuine," each one "incentivizes the creation of more bogus, anomaly-creating explanations." The result is "a loss of both ratiocinative and ethical integrity."

The belief is preserved precisely because the machinery that would test it has been dismantled.

Kuczynski 039 — PASS
Question: Why does self-deception not require a mind divided into separate persons?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 287

EXACT ANSWER
Self-deception does not require a divided mind because the division it needs is not between persons but between having a mental content and knowing that one has it.

I make this explicit: "one must distinguish having a given propositional attitude (or, more generally, mental content) from knowing that one has it." That single distinction does all the work. A person can feel a certain way toward something without knowing it, because "knowing that one feels that way involves making a judgment about one's mental state." No second agent is required—only a gap between the attitude and the judgment about it.

Second, propositional attitudes never appear in consciousness directly. As I put it, "propositional attitudes never make an appearance in consciousness in propria persona---or, rather, they do so, but only in the indirect sense in which rocks and trees do." What is conscious is "at best some representative or derivative of a propositional attitude." So Smith can have a surge of anger toward his dad on Monday without that anger presenting itself as such.

Third, self-deception presupposes a kind of knowledge. I note that "self-deception seems to presuppose that, at some level, one has knowledge of the thing of which one lacks conscious knowledge." This is not two persons—one knowing, one deceived. It is one person whose attitude is expressed in consciousness only through a derivative, and who fails to form the correct judgment about it.

The failure of transparency is enough. As I argue, "all that follows is that one is not completely transparent to oneself; it doesn't follow that one can self-deceive." But with the two additions above—the attitude/judgment distinction and the indirect presence of attitudes in consciousness—self-deception becomes possible without positing homunculi or split selves.

Kuczynski 040 — PASS
Question: What role does anxiety play when repression breaks down?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 282

EXACT ANSWER
Anxiety is not the return of the repressed. It is the signal that repression is failing—that your own thought-processes are following clues you deliberately refused to follow.

When you repress, you don't destroy the belief. You make "a belief's conscious representation an innocuous shell." The belief persists, inert at the level of consciousness, but still operative below it. You keep it isolated so it cannot engage with your other beliefs.

But the mind is not unified. "The mind is not a perfectly unified entity; it consists of various parts that communicate with each other only very imperfectly." That fragmentation is what makes repression possible—and what makes its breakdown so destabilizing.

Here is the mechanism. You suspect something. You don't want to know it. So you steer your thinking away. But "suspicion isn't knowledge; and there's nothing paradoxical about not wanting to pursue suspicions that one suspects would, if pursued, lead to knowledge one would rather not have." The suspicion doesn't vanish. It keeps generating pressure.

The anxiety that erupts "isn't the result of some hitherto unconscious knowledge entering consciousness. It is the result one's own thought-processes, despite one's attempt to stymie them, following up on clues that it had good reason to believe would lead to a belief that one doesn't want to have."

Consider Mac. He was in over his head, but couldn't admit it without shattering his self-concept. "People have panic attacks when they are in a situation in which they are actually threatened but where they cannot admit as much without having to forfeit an inflated self-concept."

The anxiety is the cost of the lie. It is what happens when reality presses against a self-concept that cannot absorb it.

Kuczynski 041 — PASS
Question: What does Libet's experiment actually show about free will?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Causality
Word count: 368

EXACT ANSWER
The Libet experiment shows that your conscious decisions are not yours. They are yours to behold, but not to make.

This is the crucial point, and it is routinely obscured. Philosophers ask "what does the experiment show about free will?" when they should ask "what does the experiment show?" The first question is open-ended and demands that we evaluate the results on their own terms; the second demands that we evaluate perspicuous experimental data in terms of the vagaries of a concept. As I've argued, "nothing is gained, and much is lost, by trying to press RLE into the service of a pre-existing philosophical agenda."

What the results actually show is stark. "What we believe to be choices that are initiated in consciousness are not initiated in consciousness; they are initiated prior to any conscious awareness of them and also far outside any such awareness." The readiness potential is already underway before you appear on the scene. By the time you make an appearance, the decision has already been made.

This is not merely the old idea that choices have causes. "It is proof that we what we thought were choices aren't. It is also proof that the center of consciousness that a person regards as the nucleus of his very self, and of his agency in particular, isn't."

Consider what this does to compatibilism. Compatibilism says you are free even though you are determined. But the experiment shows something worse than determination: "what is ominous about the Libet Experiment... is not so much that it shows your choices to be predetermined but that it shows them not to be yours."

And it is more radical than Freud. "Freud regarded this central conscious authority as, indeed, a central authority, albeit a weak one—like a ruler who, though weak, is not a puppet. But Libet's work shows that in the very contexts where this ruler least appears to be a puppet, that is precisely what he is."

So: does the experiment refute free will? That question is malformed. It shows that "one's center of consciousness—this being what people intuitively regard as the center of their very selves—has a much more subordinate role in decision-making than people, including philosophers, assume."

Kuczynski 042 — PASS
Question: Why does neural activity preceding conscious awareness not disprove agency?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 336

EXACT ANSWER
The neural activity preceding conscious awareness does not disprove agency because it does not constitute a separable causal event. "The suite events that Libet is studying---the event-chain comprising RP and W---does not consist of 'parts'." Therefore "we cannot say that some early 'part' of it is responsible for some later 'part' of it." RPI is not a cause standing behind the intention; it is "simply the first part of a single, indivisible unit of a process that is temporally indissoluble but only the last part of which rises above the level of conscious awareness."

This is the crux. Critics treat the readiness potential as though it were a hidden agent pulling the strings before "I" arrive on the scene. But there is no homunculus in the motor cortex. The brain activity and the conscious intention are phases of one process, not two competing agents.

Even granting the data, the inference to epiphenomenalism fails. "Most philosophers, if not all, have taken the position that, even if that data is legitimate, it doesn't warrant a revision of any of frameworks within which philosophers have traditionally attempted to understand the nature of human 'freedom'." Some have argued "that the brain-activity preceding W is not causative of W and, therefore, that W is indeed the operative intention."

And crucially, consciousness retains a regulatory role. "RLE2 are consistent with the supposition that W 'polices' the subject's intention to move." In general, "our 'conscious intentions'—in other words, our conscious awareness of our intentions—have the power to check and possibly otherwise modify the execution of those intentions."

So the subject who called his decision "utterly 'free'" was not deluded. The RLE "are profoundly important" and "do in fact warrant a revision of many conceits about the mind—specifically about agency and the role therein of the 'self'." But revising our conceits is not abolishing agency. It is clarifying where agency actually lives: not in a ghostly moment of conscious fiat, but in the ongoing, integrated process by which intention is formed, monitored, and checked.

Kuczynski 043 — PASS
Question: What is wrong with identifying a decision with awareness of deciding?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 6
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Causality; Do We Think in Words?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 254

EXACT ANSWER
The identification is false, and the Libet data prove it. What you experience as your decision is not your decision—it is an awareness of a decision already made by some other party.

The reasoning is straightforward. Readiness potential is "obviously the proximal psychological cause of the action." An intention is the proximal psychological cause of the action. Therefore RP is the intention. Therefore the conscious event W "is not the intention; it is an awareness of the intention."

So when you identify your decision with your awareness of deciding, you are mistaking a report for the thing reported. As I put it, "the conscious events that we believe to be our intentions are not our intentions." They are "mere awarenesses of the corresponding intentions."

This is more radical than Freud. Freud held that consciousness is "like a monarch whose power, though considerable, is limited"—a weak ruler, but still a ruler. Libet shows the ruler is a puppet. The conscious event "is not even involved in that decision; it is on the sidelines."

Why does the error persist? Because these acts are, after a fashion, decisions—"decisions to green-light an existing intention." And if they were experienced as what they actually are, that "might overburden consciousness." One makes "innumerably many choices within the space of a single minute."

So the identification is not a harmless slip. It is a systematic misdescription of the architecture. The "center of consciousness that a person regards as the nucleus of his very self, and of his agency in particular, isn't."

Kuczynski 044 — PASS
Question: How does unconscious preparation relate to a conscious action?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Do We Think in Words?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 236

EXACT ANSWER
The conscious act is the last link in a causal chain, not the first. "What we believe to be choices that are initiated in consciousness are not initiated in consciousness; they are initiated prior to any conscious awareness of them and also far outside any such awareness."

This is the core of my position. The will does not originate action; it registers an outcome that has already been prepared beneath the threshold of awareness.

Consider the architecture. I distinguish "two kinds of awareness: propositional and objectual." My awareness of the tightness in my shoes is objectual—direct, non-conceptual. My knowledge *that* my shoes are tight is propositional. The unconscious operates in the objectual register long before anything surfaces as a proposition I can avow.

The mechanism is the barrier between conscious and unconscious minds. When that barrier is "too rigid," it "prevents the conscious mind from drawing on the power in the unconscious mind." When relaxed, the conscious mind draws on that power—but it does not author it.

This is why I say "religious sentiment is possible only when the barrier between consciousness and unconsciousness is relaxed," and that "the religious person hasn't discovered God so much as he has projected him." The same structure governs action: what feels like initiation is projection of a prepared impulse into the theater of consciousness.

So the relation is one of *disclosure*, not *causation*. Consciousness illuminates; it does not ignite.

Kuczynski 045 — PASS
Question: Why does Alcoholics Anonymous work?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 278

EXACT ANSWER
AA works because it exploits a structural feature of the alcoholic's mind: the will is already broken, so recovery cannot come from willing harder.

The alcoholic is "internally divided: one part of him wants to drink; the other wants to live." Self-knowledge is useless here. As I put it, "will and self-knowledge—the two cornerstones of self-determination—are useless." The alcoholic "manages to deceive himself into thinking that he can do so" even when he knows he cannot. So the program does not appeal to will. It bypasses it.

The mechanism is surrender. The alcoholic "must acknowledge that his will is defunct and surrender to a higher power." That power is a projection of his superego. "The alcoholic's higher power is a projection of his superego. By surrendering to it, he realigns himself with his superego, and the resulting internal unity gives him peace of mind and strength of will."

Why project it outward rather than look inward? Because "he has spent years justifying his degenerate conduct to himself, making it impossible for him to find anything other than rationalizations when he looks inwards for moral guidance." The conscience is intact but buried under rationalization. Projecting it as an external God restores its authority.

The leap of faith is "justified by the consequences." And crucially, "the alcoholic's ability to find his power does not in any way correlate with his pre-existing religious beliefs"—the agnostic benefits as much as the believer.

Finally, the Steps are "operationalized through constant action, as opposed to mere intellectual acceptance." Working them makes the alcoholic "more action-oriented and less focused on his emotions and less egocentric generally." That is the cure: not insight, but redirected conduct.

Kuczynski 046 — PASS
Question: What psychological function is served by admitting powerlessness in Alcoholics Anonymous?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 16
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 232

EXACT ANSWER
Admitting powerlessness serves to deflate the ego and thereby weaken the narcissism that sustains the alcoholic's self-deception. The alcoholic is "internally divided: one part of him wants to drink; the other wants to live," and the drinking part keeps winning because the ego insists it can control what it cannot.

My model treats the higher power as "a projection of his superego." When the alcoholic admits he is powerless, he stops defending the rationalizations that let him drink. He "must project his superego in order to surrender to it owing to the fact that he has spent years justifying his degenerate conduct to himself, making it impossible for him to find anything other than rationalizations when he looks inwards for moral guidance."

So the psychological function is realignment. "By surrendering to it, he realigns himself with his superego, and the resulting internal unity gives him peace of mind and strength of will." The ego, "after all, is about control, and surrendering is forfeiture of ego."

This is why the move works even for atheists. "Believers and non-believers alike have ego-ideals," and the power surrendered to is "his idealized self---his ego-ideal, as we might put it." Acknowledging powerlessness "is itself a way of making him less narcissistic and, therefore, less egocentric."

The result is a strengthened will, not a weakened one. The alcoholic trades "indolent, childish ego in exchange for action-positive, adult non-ego."

Kuczynski 047 — PASS
Question: Why is surrendering to a higher power essential in Alcoholics Anonymous?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 20
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 288

EXACT ANSWER
Surrendering to a higher power is essential because the alcoholic's will has been completely shattered. He is internally divided: one part of him wants to drink; the other wants to live. The part that wants to drink is winning, and it continues to win until the choice is quit or die. At that point, "he must acknowledge that his will is defunct and surrender to a higher power."

The mechanism is what interests me. It is suggestive that each person has his own personal higher power, and what this suggests is that "this higher power is a part of that person's mind: in surrendering to a higher power, the alcoholic is really surrendering to some part of himself." My model is precise: "The alcoholic's higher power is a projection of his superego. By surrendering to it, he realigns himself with his superego, and the resulting internal unity gives him peace of mind and strength of will."

Why must he project it rather than simply consult it? Because he has spent years justifying his degenerate conduct to himself, "making it impossible for him to find anything other than rationalizations when he looks inwards for moral guidance." The superego must be externalized before it can be obeyed.

The surrender also does structural work on the ego. "The ego, after all, is about control, and surrendering is forfeiture of ego." Acknowledging his own powerlessness is itself a way of making him less narcissistic, and the recovering alcoholic's shift from a passive to an active relationship to the world serves to keep his ego in check.

This is why the leap of faith is justified by its consequences. It is not the content of the belief that saves him—it is the realignment.

Kuczynski 048 — PASS
Question: What is the difference between changing oneself and changing one's self?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Part II; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 231

EXACT ANSWER
The difference is between how one is and who one is. A person can change himself, but he cannot change his self.

My position is that "one's identity is predetermined." Experience does not determine who one is; it determines how one is who one is. So when I speak of changing oneself, I mean altering the surface—habits, beliefs, behaviors, the operational layer of a life. When I speak of changing one's self, I mean attempting to alter the underlying identity itself. That is not transformation. That is psychological suicide.

I have argued that "Sally is committing psychological suicide. She is trying to change who she is, not how she is. But one can't change who one is."

The empirical evidence supports this. I cite "studies of identical twins who were raised in different environments. One twin would be raised in a mansion, the other in a hovel." One went to "an elite boarding school, the other to the local public school." Yet "such twins were identical in respect of their IQs, degree of spirituality, and to what degree, and in what manner, they were social or anti-social."

The conclusion follows: "Changes to oneself, if they are not to render one's self defunct, must occur within limits set by one's inborn biological structure."

So change of self is bounded. Change of oneself is possible—but only within the architecture one was born with.

Kuczynski 049 — PASS
Question: Why is psychological identity constrained by inborn biological structure?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 4
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Do We Think in Words?
Word count: 294

EXACT ANSWER
Psychological identity is constrained by inborn biological structure because one's identity is predetermined—experience determines *how* one is who one is, not *who* one is. As I put it, "One's identity is predetermined. Experience doesn't determine who one is; it determines how one is who one is."

The evidence for this is striking. I cite "studies of identical twins who were raised in different environments. One twin would be raised in a mansion, the other in a hovel... Such twins were identical in respect of their IQs, degree of spirituality, and to what degree, and in what manner, they were social or anti-social." If environment alone fixed identity, such twins would diverge radically. They don't.

The mechanism is that "A person can change himself, but he cannot change his self. Changes to oneself, if they are not to render one's self defunct, must occur within limits set by one's inborn biological structure."

This extends to values. "The range of admissible value systems is limited by immutable facts about our psychology (and biology)." Chomsky showed that "any humanly learnable language satisfies rather narrowly defined formal constraints," and the same logic applies to value: "the range of psychologically (and biologically) admissible values is limited."

The stronger claim follows: "there are limits not only to what kinds of value systems you can happily accept, but to what you kinds of value systems you can accept at all." You cannot change "how you emotionally react to situations," and since "you can change your value system only by changing your emotional architecture, then you cannot (except in trivial respects) change your value system at all."

This is why Sally's project—"psychological suicide"—fails. "She is trying to change who she is, not how she is. But one can't change who one is."

Kuczynski 050 — PASS
Question: Why is the attempt to empiricize psychology not identical with making psychology scientific?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 4
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 261

EXACT ANSWER
The attempt to empiricize psychology is not identical with making psychology scientific because science is not about sticking to the facts—it is about organizing them.

The empiricist's injunction that we "stick to the facts" is either the triviality that correct theories are fact-consistent or it's the absurdity that one can model data without extrapolating. Science, properly understood, is "about organizing the facts, and it is therefore about positing hidden structures to restore to nature the continuities of which observation strips it."

This is why the empiricization of psychology is sterile. The empiricist treats psychological processes as if they were merely observable data points, ignoring that "psychological processes are brain-dependent" and that introspection is itself "an act of conceptualization, and not of theoretically innocent reportage."

The empiricist's error runs deeper. When empiricists like Hume overlook that introspection is conceptual through and through, or when Husserl tries to rescue empiricism by "stripping introspection of its conceptual component," they fail—because "since introspection is conception, this approach is doomed to fail."

The real distinction is epistemic. "Epistemically speaking, physics and physiology model the same facts," so they can be reconciled even if neither reduces to the other. But "psychology and physics don't converge on any one set of facts." The psychologist's data are not the physicist's data, and no amount of empirical rigor will change that.

So empiricizing psychology does not scientize it. It merely confuses the demand for theoretical organization with the demand for observational purity. My aim has been to help "psychology begin to undo the damage that empiricism has done to it."

Kuczynski 051 — PASS
Question: Are there any genuinely non-extensional contexts?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 5
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Part II; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 315

EXACT ANSWER
No. There are no intensional contexts. Every context is substitution-tolerant.

My argument is straightforward. When two expressions that appear to refer to the same individual cannot be inter-substituted, they actually refer to different propositions. That is the whole of it. "Thus, there are no intensional contexts. (In other words, all contexts are substitution-tolerant.)"

Consider the standard apparent counterexamples. Take the sentences involving the woman who is prettier than every other woman. The real meanings are not what they seem. What is actually meant is "it is necessarily the case that any woman who is prettier than every other woman is prettier than every other woman," and separately, "it is necessarily the case that any woman who is prettier than every other woman is also more evil than every other person."

The first attributes the property of being necessarily true to one proposition. The second expresses that same property to some other proposition. "Since those propositions are distinct, the fact that the one proposition is necessarily true doesn't entail that the same is true of the other; and there is thus violation of Leibniz's Law."

So the appearance of non-extensionality dissolves. What looked like one context is really two, each operating on a different proposition.

The term "intensional" remains useful, but only as shorthand. "Thus used, an 'intensional context' in which expressions that otherwise refer to individuals (e.g., 'Benjamin Franklin') refer to propositions." An expression occurring there does so intensionally. A context that isn't intensional is extensional, and expressions in it do so extensionally.

The Slingshot literature gets this backwards. It assumes co-referring terms can fail to preserve reference in "non-extensional" contexts, then treats that failure as a datum. But strictly, what is true is only that "in extensional contexts, intersubstituting co-referring terms doesn't change reference." The alleged counterexamples are not counterexamples to extensionality—they are cases where the terms were never co-referring in the first place.

Kuczynski 052 — FAIL
Question: What is the difference between semantics and presemantics?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Part II; Do We Think in Words?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 240
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
The indexed corpus does not contain a direct answer to this question. I have no passage that defines "presemantics" or draws the distinction you're asking about, and I won't manufacture one.

What the corpus does contain are arguments about *differences* of various kinds, and it may be useful to say what those are—not as an answer to your question, but as adjacent material.

I distinguish differences that are "one of degree" from differences that are not. I argue that "the difference between a clock that seldom stops and one that often stops is purely statistical," and that "the same is true of the difference between a witness who seldom lies and one who often lies." From this I conclude that "the difference between knowledge and mere belief is not statistical."

I make a parallel move with laws. The difference between a cyanide-intolerance generalization and a shellfish-intolerance generalization "is not to be understood in statistical terms," because "it is inherent in what it is to be physiologically human (or otherwise mammalian) that a human being be cyanide intolerant."

I also distinguish immanent from transeunt causation: "immanent causation is, whereas transeunt causation is not, structure-preserving," and "transeunt causation involves the cessation of a structure-preserving causal series."

These are distinctions among causal, nomological, and epistemic categories. None of them is a distinction between semantics and presemantics. I cannot tell you what that distinction is on the basis of what I have written here.

[Part II] In natural language, quantified sentences are pseudo-objectual statements. They appear to be about objects, but are really about properties.
[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] Propositions are intensions (though not all intensions are propositions—e.g., the propositional function x is a human being is not a proposition, even though it is an intension).
[Do We Think in Words?] Some kinds of information (e.g., visual information) seem inherently incapable of being fully expressed by sentences of English or of any other natural language. Also, sentences have very different structures from pictures, even when the sentences and the pictures overlap in content.
[Do We Think in Words?] Meanings are themselves symbols. So unless we already know what it is to understand symbols, positing symbol-meanings doesn’t help us understand the nature of symbol-comprehension. Positing symbol meanings in the hopes of explaining symbol-comprehension is like positing little people inside people’s heads in the hopes of explaining how people think.
[Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence] According to one point of view (which I, along with many authors, hold) word-types are properties, even though word-tokens are not properties and are instead instances of properties.
[Part II] Changes in what we believe affect what we infer from statements; but they don’t categorically change the meanings of those statements.
[Do We Think in Words?] The expressions of Smithese1, then, don’t mean whatever Smith thinks they mean. If his opinions assign new meanings to X1...Xn, those opinions are creating a new language that happens to be orthographically and phonetically coincident with Smithese1; those opinions aren’t assigning new meanings to expressions constituting some existing language.
[Do We Think in Words?] We can obviously have thoughts that we are incapable of putting into words. This shows that we grasp those thoughts in some way other than by grasping the English sentences that express them. Also, there are occasions where, although we can put into words what we are thinking, doing so requires great effort: we have to think long and hard about which words to use. This shows that thinking is often a prerequisite to using language, which in turn shows that at least some thinking is non-linguistic.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Custom-driven behavior is not rule-governed behavior. The former therefore isn't true or false, or otherwise right or wrong, and it therefore isn't linguistic.
[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] States of affairs (or “facts,” as they are sometimes called) do not, as Ian Hacking (1975) long ago put it, have the same kinds of “articulations” as propositions.

Kuczynski 053 — PASS
Question: Does the necessary a posteriori survive the semantics/presemantics distinction?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Part II; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Do We Think in Words?
Word count: 317

EXACT ANSWER
No. The necessary a posteriori does not survive the distinction between semantics and presemantics.

My argument is straightforward. "Kripke's arguments for these views appear probative only so long as one fails to distinguish between semantics and presemantics—between the literal meanings of sentences, on the one hand, and the information on the basis of which one identifies those literal meanings, on the other."

This distinction is fatal to the Kripkean picture. Once you separate the literal meaning of a sentence from the information by which you identify that meaning, the alleged necessity of the a posteriori collapses. What looked like a necessary truth known only through experience turns out to be an artifact of conflating two different things.

The same failure infects the companion claim. "It is widely held that one can sometimes rationally accept propositions of the form P and not-P." And "it is also widely held that there are necessary a posteriori truths." Both views, I contend, rest on the same conflation.

Consider what happens when the distinction is drawn properly. The semantic content of a sentence—its literal meaning—is one thing. The presemantic information that fixes our grip on that content is another. Kripke's examples trade on the appearance that the two are inseparable.

They are not. "The acceptance of necessary a posteriori truths relies on a failure to distinguish semantics from presemantics."

Kant made a parallel error. "What led Kant astray was a failure to distinguish the..."—and here the same structural mistake recurs. He treated knowledge of how we must perceive the world as a priori, when in fact "even this knowledge turns to be a posteriori."

The lesson generalizes. Whenever necessity and aposteriority appear to coincide, look for a hidden equivocation between what a sentence means and how we come to know what it means. The necessary a posteriori is not a genuine category. It is a symptom of not having drawn the distinction.

Kuczynski 054 — FAIL
Question: Does Frege's puzzle require a theory of sense?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Do We Think in Words?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality
Word count: 217
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
No. Frege's puzzle does not require a theory of sense.

The puzzle is a datum: identity statements like "a=a" and "a=b" differ in cognitive significance. The question is what explains that difference. Frege posits senses—modes of presentation—as the explanation. But the puzzle itself only constrains the explanation; it does not dictate one.

My own framework handles this without senses. I hold that "analytic truths, even when not obviously so, express dependence-relations." Identity statements are analytic in exactly this way: "1+1=2" and "1+1≠3" are my own examples of analytic truths, and they express dependence-relations rather than tracking senses.

The cognitive difference between "a=a" and "a=b" is a difference in what dependence-relations the thinker grasps. And such truths are known through conceptual analysis: "Some dependence-relations are known, not through sensory observation, but through conceptual analysis." No mode of presentation is needed—only the structure of the dependence-relation itself.

Frege's senses are posited to do explanatory work that dependence-relations already do. As I argue against Lewis's parallel move, "There is no reason, apart from Lewis's analysis, to believe that there are other worlds." The same applies here: apart from Frege's analysis, there is no reason to posit senses.

The puzzle is real. The sense-theoretic solution is one option among several. My dependence-relation account is another, and it is the leaner one.

Kuczynski 055 — PASS
Question: Is indirect referentialism coherent?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Part II; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Causality; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 265

EXACT ANSWER
Indirect referentialism is coherent. The apparent paradox dissolves once you distinguish literal meaning from communicative content.

The core of my view is this: "Supposing proper names are mere labels, the literal meaning of sentences with co-referring names is the same." So "'Hesperus has phi' has the same literal meaning as 'Phosphorous has phi,'" and "'Hesperus is identical with Phosphorous' has the same literal meaning as 'Hesperus is identical with Hesperus.'"

The Fregean objection—that one sentence "communicates a significant astronomical discovery" while the other "is an empty truism"—only seems devastating if you conflate two different things. It isn't. "What an occurrence of a sentence communicates to somebody isn't always confined to its literal meaning." Indeed, it "isn't necessarily even inclusive of its literal meaning."

That is the key move. "Communicative differences arise from how people ascertain meanings, not from literal meaning differences." More precisely: "Those differences are to be explained not by supposing that those sentences differ in respect of literal meaning," but "in terms of facts about how people ascertain the literal meanings of the sentences that they encounter."

The mechanism is straightforward. "To hear a sentence is not to hear a proposition. One must make one's way from the physical noise that one hears." That journey—from noise to proposition—can differ even when the destination is identical. Hence "the fallacy that underlies this supposed paradox is easily exposed."

So the coherence of indirect referentialism rests on a single distinction: same literal meaning, different routes of access. Once you see that communicative content can outrun or diverge from literal meaning, the Fregean puzzle loses its force.

Kuczynski 056 — PASS
Question: Does Russell's Theory of Descriptions correctly analyze definite descriptions?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 8
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Causality; Do We Think in Words?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 263

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. Russell's central insight is correct: definite descriptions are not referring terms. They are quantifiers.

The argument is straightforward. "Definite descriptions aren't referring terms. No definite description refers to anything." The meaning of "the inventor of Velcro" isn't given by saying who it refers to, but by saying what whole sentences containing it mean. This is the contextual definition Russell identified.

Consider negative existentials. "The monster under little Timmy's bed doesn't exist" is true. But if that description referred to something, it couldn't be true. As I put it, "N1 is true. But if 'the monster under little Timmy's bed' referred to something, it wouldn't be true, since there would then exist some monster under little Timmy's bed to which that definite description referred."

The reparsing solves this. What the sentence really says is that "the property of being a monster under little Timmy's bed isn't instantiated." That property exists—there is a way something would have to be to be such a monster—but nothing has it.

The same treatment applies to "the fountain of youth exists." Reparsed, it becomes "the property of being a fountain of youth is uniquely instantiated." And Ponce de Leon's belief becomes: "Ponce de Leon believes that the property of being a fountain of youth is uniquely instantiated."

The linguistic markers confirm this. "The word 'the' obviously indicates existence (unlike the word 'no') and also uniqueness (unlike the word 'a')." Both features are captured by the quantificational analysis.

So Russell is right. Definite descriptions are "defined, not denotatively, but contextually—that is, by saying what whole sentences containing it mean."

Kuczynski 057 — PASS
Question: Are proper names rigid designators?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 4
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Part II; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Do We Think in Words?
Word count: 314

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. Proper names are rigid designators.

My argument runs through a semantic test. Consider a world W that is semantically just like ours except that, in it, Al Gore, not Barack Obama, is the U.S. President in 2009. In W, "the U.S. President in 2009" would refer to Al Gore. So even though that expression would have the semantics that it has in our world, it wouldn't pick out the same thing that it picks out in our world.

That is the mark of a non-rigid designator. "The LMS," like "the current U.S. President," is not a rigid designator; it refers to different things in semantically identical situations. It used to refer to Carter; now it refers to Obama. But the semantic rules governing that expression haven't changed in the least in the meantime.

Proper names behave differently. If x is the planet that in our world is referred to as "Venus," "Venus" would refer to x in W*. "Venus," like "Barack Obama," is thus a rigid designator. If x is what "Venus" in fact refers to, x is what "Venus" would refer to in any world semantically like ours.

In general, proper names are rigid designators. "Hesperus" and "Phosphorous" are proper names. Therefore, unlike "the LMS" and "the FES," they are rigid designators. So as long as the semantic rules of English haven't changed, there is some one object x such that each refers to x.

This has a consequence for identity claims. A sentence of the form "A is identical with B" expresses an identity claim only if "A" and "B" are rigid designators. Thus "Hesperus is identical with Phosphorous" is an identity claim. And so is "water is H2O" and so is "Mark Twain is Samuel Clemens."

It's obvious that, for any object x, the proposition x is identical with x is necessarily true. That necessity is exactly what rigidity secures.

Kuczynski 058 — PASS
Question: What does Kripke's Pierre puzzle actually show about belief?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Part II; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Do We Think in Words?; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 263

EXACT ANSWER
The Pierre puzzle shows that belief is not a relation to a single proposition with a fixed cognitive value. It shows that semantic meaning and communicated content come apart.

My analysis runs like this. "Sentences like (7) and (9) have the same literal meaning at the semantic level." Yet "the propositions communicated by (7) and (9) under specific circumstances are completely different and do not entail each other." That is the whole puzzle in two sentences.

Pierre assents to one sentence and dissents from another. If belief were just a relation to literal meaning, he would be irrational. He is not. "(7) communicates (11), whereas (9) communicates (16)." And "(11) and (16) are completely different propositions. Neither so much as entails the other."

So what does Pierre actually believe? Two distinct propositions. "Thus, I can, without necessarily being irrational, assent to the one without assenting to the other."

This is not a defect in Pierre. It is a feature of how belief works. "Rationality is determined by how a person handles the information at their disposal, not just the semantic content of the sentences they encounter." Pierre handles his information correctly. His two beliefs are anchored in different bodies of evidence and different modes of presentation.

The lesson is that there is "a discrepancy between semantic meaning and the cognitive value or propositional content communicated by a sentence in a given context." Belief tracks the latter, not the former. Kripke's puzzle is not a paradox about belief. It is a demonstration that belief is context-sensitive in a way that literal meaning is not.

Kuczynski 059 — PASS
Question: Do substitution failures in belief contexts prove intensionality?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 4
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Causality; Part II; Do We Think in Words?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 323

EXACT ANSWER
No. Substitution failures in belief contexts do not prove intensionality. They prove that the expressions in question refer to different propositions.

My position is that "there are no intensional contexts. (In other words, all contexts are substitution-tolerant.)" When two expressions that appear to refer to the same individual cannot be inter-substituted, "they actually refer to different propositions." The failure is not a feature of the context — it is a feature of the referents.

Consider the classic case. Take (3) and (4). Their real meanings are:

"(3*) it is necessarily the case that any woman who is prettier than every other woman is prettier than every other woman."

"(4*) it is necessarily the case that any woman who is prettier than every other woman is also more evil than every other person."

(3*) attributes necessary truth to one proposition. (4*) attributes it to a different one. "Since those propositions are distinct, the fact that the one proposition is necessarily true doesn't entail that the same is true of the other; and there is thus violation of Leibniz's Law."

So the alleged counterexample collapses. What looked like a substitution failure is really a substitution of distinct propositions.

The same reasoning generalizes. "Given any context—modal, moral, causal, etc.—an argument exactly similar to the one just given shows that replacing a name occurring in that context with a co-referring name cannot possibly lead from truth to falsity, at least not at the level of semantics."

To establish extensionality, one of two contentions must hold: "(A) Definite descriptions are not referring terms. (B) Even if definite descriptions are in fact referring terms, intersubstituting co-referring definite descriptions does not, contrary to initial appearances, change truth-value." I argue for (B).

The term "intensional" remains useful only as shorthand. An "intensional context" is one in which expressions that otherwise refer to individuals refer to propositions. That is a description of what the expressions do, not evidence of a special context-type.

Kuczynski 060 — PASS
Question: Is conceptual atomism true?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Do We Think in Words?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 245

EXACT ANSWER
No. Conceptual atomism is false.

The doctrine requires that there be simple, non-composite truths—conceptual "atoms" out of which all other truths are constructed. But there are no such things. As I have argued, there are "no 'atoms' of nature—no non-composite truths out of which other truths can be constructed or from which they can be derived."

This is the core of my objection. The atomist needs foundational simples. They don't exist.

Consider what atomism demands. It requires that complex propositions decompose into basic ones, and that these basic ones are self-standing. But decomposition always terminates in further structure, not in atoms. The regress never bottoms out in simples.

My summary of the matter is blunt: "Atoms, in the sense in which the philosophy of logical atomism requires them to exist, don't."

The atomist might retreat to a weaker claim—that some truths are basic even if not atomic. But that concedes the point. Basic-for-us is not the same as non-composite in itself. The atomist needs the latter.

I also deny the underlying epistemology. Knowledge is not built from atomic units of acquaintance. "Awareness of objects does not constitute knowledge; knowledge consists of truths about objects." Truths, not atoms of experience, are the currency of knowledge. And truths are structured, relational, composite.

So conceptual atomism fails on two fronts. There are no atomic truths to serve as foundations, and there is no atomic awareness to serve as their epistemic ground. The picture is wrong at the bottom.

Kuczynski 061 — PASS
Question: Is meaning determined by use?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 14
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Do We Think in Words?
Word count: 279

EXACT ANSWER
No. Meaning is not use, and the doctrine that says otherwise is incoherent.

Wittgenstein's position is that "meaning is use." I reject it. The reason is simple: "A meaningless burst of noise isn't an expression." A cough or a guffaw has no linguistic meaning. It becomes an expression only once it already has a meaning. So how it is used cannot be what gives it its meaning.

This is decisive. "Since anything that is a linguistic expression ipso facto has a meaning (in the relevant, linguistic sense), there are no expressions to be used before noises, ink-marks, etc., have been assigned meanings." There is no expression-use prior to expression-meaning. So expression-use cannot constitute expression-meaning. As I put it, "'Meaningful expression' is a pleonasm."

The same failure infects conceptual role semantics. Its advocates claim that what a sentence means is fixed by what we infer from it. But this reverses the order. We infer "an even number is less than five" from "two is less than five" because the latter already means what it means. If we discovered tomorrow that Aristotle wrote plays, the sentence "Aristotle wrote the Nichomachean Ethics" would not thereby change its semantic meaning.

Meaning guides use, not the reverse. "Our knowledge of what words mean is what guides our linguistic behavior." I know what "hug" means and what "discuss" means; that is why I say "I want to discuss the exam," not "I want to hug the exam." "If meaning were use, meaning couldn't guide use. But it obviously does."

There is a difference between causal determination and identity. Use may causally assign a new meaning. That is not the same as use being the meaning.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS

Kuczynski 062 — PASS
Question: Is linguistic understanding a body of knowledge or an ability?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 249

EXACT ANSWER
Linguistic understanding is an ability, not a body of knowledge. The two are not the same, and conflating them is the central error.

My position is that grasping a principle is knowing how to apply it. As I put it, "the grasp SR is for me to know what to do when approaching a red light. Knowledge of how to apply SR is built into knowledge of SR. Knowledge of secondary principles is no part of the story."

This is the crux. The conventional wisdom holds that "thinking is a psychological process." I deny that the relevant difference between someone who understands and someone who does not is psychological at all.

Consider two individuals. "Let A and B be individuals of the first and second types, respectively. (So A understands why F(13) = 169, whereas B does not.)" There is a relevant difference between them—otherwise both would understand or neither would. "But that difference is not of a strictly psychological nature."

The objection is predictable: surely I grasp the function F(x) = x² but must still figure out how to apply it to 1,198,976. My reply invokes "Chomsky's (1965) distinction between competence and performance. Competence is ability."

So understanding is competence—an ability. It is not a stockpile of propositions one consults. It is "a knowledge of how to apply" what one grasps, built into the grasp itself.

The mistake is treating understanding as a body of knowledge stored and then deployed. It is not. It is the ability itself.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS
