[Do We Think in Words?] Wittgenstein’s view that thinking consists of manipulating expressions is inconsistent with the fact that, unless one can think, one cannot use expressions meaningfully. If I just bark out sounds, having no idea what they mean, I’m not in any real sense using language. For my noise-making to be speech, it must be guided by a knowledge of what those noises mean.
[Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible] So, speaking epistemologically, these psychological “intangibles” are much more tangible, and their existence infinitely less dubitable, than “tangible” things such as brains.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Operating independently of each other, Wittgenstein and Quine, both staunch empiricists, came to the same, radically counterintuitive conclusion, to wit: (QW113) When trying to figure out what another person means, one must focus only on his body movements.
[Do We Think in Words?] People who speak different languages can have the same thoughts. Monolingual Spanish speakers believe that 1 + 1 = 2, and so do monolingual English speakers. But English and Spanish speakers use different sentences to express that truth, showing that thinking that truth cannot be identical with thinking a sentence of English or Spanish or, by obvious extensions of this line of thought, any other language.
[Part II] Meaning in the psychological sense is therefore parasitic on meaning in the linguistic sense.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Custom-driven behavior is not rule-governed behavior. The former therefore isn't true or false, or otherwise right or wrong, and it therefore isn't linguistic.
[Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism] Sellars has shown that in non-standard conditions, one must rely (however unconsciously) on background knowledge if one is to learn, through one’s senses, how things are in the external world.
[Part II] Where complex expressions are concerned, there is no limit to how much literal and understood meaning may diverge from each other. But where simple expressions are concerned, literal and understood meaning must coalesce.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] To understand an expression is to know what it means. Some take this to entail that, given any meaningful expression E, there is some object M that is E's meaning. But this is not the right view.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] Just as Searle believes, cognitive processes are realized by physiological processes. But it doesn't follow, and it's a priori improbable, that the psychological relations between physiological events can be understood in physiological terms.
[Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology] The more one knows about the world, the more one can control it. The more one knows about oneself, the more one can control oneself.
[Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism] Berkeley’s major point is that no sensory experience by itself tells one anything. Any such experience has to be put in the context of a lot of background knowledge if it is to bear any determinate message and if, therefore, it is to tell one anything about the external world.
[Do We Think in Words?] Thus, there are languages only where there are right and wrong ways to use expressions. Since it is only where several people are using a language that there are right and wrong ways of using its expressions, it is only where there are several people that there are languages: all languages are public.
[Part II] Nothing meaningless is an expression. For this reason, the concept of an expression must be understood in terms of the concept of meaning, the same therefore being true of the concept of language.

Kuczynski 063 — PASS
Question: Is the computational theory of mind true?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Do We Think in Words?; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 228

EXACT ANSWER
No. The computational theory of mind is false as a complete account, because it mistakes a fragment of cognition for the whole.

My argument rests on a distinction between recursive and non-recursive capacities. Computationalism holds that "Mind = recursive computation," and functionalism extends this: "Mental states = computational states." But these frameworks run into a wall. "Qualia, intentionality, consciousness resist formalization." That resistance is not a temporary technical limitation. It is structural.

What the computationalist misses is that "mental capacities include non-recursive elements." Pattern recognition is the paradigm case. It is "neural but non-algorithmic." You cannot decompose it into recursive steps without remainder. And so "thought transcends computation."

This does not mean neuroscience is useless. Far from it. "Neuroscience studies non-recursive processes." The mind is real, embodied, and investigable. It is simply not identical to a Turing machine.

The deeper error is the assumption that all mental structure must be recursive. That assumption drives the computationalist program, and it fails at exactly the points where mind matters most: meaning, experience, and aboutness.

So my verdict is this: computational models capture fragments of cognition—perhaps large fragments—but "the mind is not purely computational." The non-recursive capacities are not epiphenomena. They are "real and essential." Any theory that cannot accommodate them is not a theory of mind. It is a theory of a proper part of mind, mistaken for the whole.

Kuczynski 064 — PASS
Question: Is any mentation subpersonal?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 13
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 219

EXACT ANSWER
Yes, and the distinction is essential to everything I argue in *Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology*.

There are two very different kinds of unconsciousness. I distinguish "the personal (Freudian) unconscious and the impersonal (Chomskyan) unconscious." The Freudian unconscious is merely access-unconscious—its contents "often used to be access-conscious and often will be so again." Given psychoanalytic intervention, you could become introspectively aware of them.

The subpersonal is different in kind. It comprises the mental events that "mediate language-learning and comprehension," and "No amount of psychoanalytic intervention could make you introspectively aware of them." They are "deeply" unconscious in Chomsky's sense. Chomsky deserves credit "for being the first to delineate the concept of subpersonal thought."

The crucial asymmetry: "Subpersonal mentation is inherently intelligent. Personal mentation is inherently unintelligent." Consciousness "is capable only of iconic representation." It mediates picture-thought, which "isn't thought at all." Whatever intelligence appears at the personal level "is bequeathed to it by the subpersonal."

So the answer is not merely yes—it is that the subpersonal stratum is where rationality actually lives. "Rationality is personal, as opposed to subpersonal, intelligence"—but only because the subpersonal supplies the rule-governed machinery that the personal stratum can then deploy. Rover's mind "mediates deeply unconscious ideation," yet Rover himself "is not very intelligent at all." The intelligence is real; it simply isn't his.

Kuczynski 065 — FAIL
Question: Are propositional attitudes relations to sentences?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Part II; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Causality
Word count: 243
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
No. Propositional attitudes are not relations to sentences—and they are not relations to anything at all, because there are no propositional attitudes.

My argument is straightforward. "The belief that there are propositional attitudes is part of folk-psychology." We treat that framework as explanatory, but we shouldn't. "We don't put any stock in folk-physics or folk-physiology." So "ceteris paribus, we shouldn't put any stock in folk-psychology." And the decisive point: "Folk-psychology is wrong because it has yielded nothing." The conclusion follows: "Propositional attitudes don't exist."

Now, suppose someone grants the attitudes and asks whether they relate us to sentences. The answer is still no, and my work on sentence-types shows why. "No series of noises is a sentence-type: an ink-deposit is, at most, a sentence-token." Which type a token instances depends on meaning: "Given some ink-deposit, which sentence-type it tokens is a function of what it means." And meaning is not acoustic or physical: "A series of noises acoustically just like utterances of the English sentence 'snow is white' could mean anything." Hence "sentence-types are not individuated by the physical properties of their tokens."

So even if you kept the attitudes, you could not identify their objects with sentences. Beliefs, if they existed, would target propositions—truths or falsehoods—not inscriptions. "Logical dependence-relations hold among propositions," and "a proposition is a truth or a falsehood."

The question presupposes two things I reject: that attitudes exist, and that sentences could serve as their relata. Neither survives scrutiny.

Kuczynski 066 — PASS
Question: Is intentionality reducible to causal covariation?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 247

EXACT ANSWER
No. Intentionality cannot be reduced to causal covariation, and the attempt to do so collapses under the same weight that breaks the Counterfactual Analysis of Causality.

My argument runs through causal redundancy. "In general, where there is causal redundancy, causal truths cannot be converted into counterfactual truths." That is the core failure. When two mechanisms could each produce the same outcome, the covariation between cause and effect dissolves—yet the causal and intentional relation persists.

Consider the bird. "The otherwise unthreatened bird died because Smith shot it." Now suppose Smith was one of a pack of hunters, each ready to fire. The counterfactual "if Smith hadn't shot it, the bird would not have died" is no longer a consequence of the causal truth. The covariation vanishes; the causation does not.

Intentionality has the same structure. A mental state can be about its object even when the covariation between state and object is broken—by redundancy, by threshold effects, by multiple realizability. "Events whose causes are mechanisms that can be implemented in different ways demonstrate causal redundancy." The same content can be carried by different vehicles. Covariation cannot track that.

So the reductionist claim—that intentionality just is covariation—"would simply be false." It mistakes a contingent correlation for the constitutive relation.

What remains is the deeper point: "causal redundancy undermines the Counterfactual Analysis of Causality." If covariation cannot even capture causation, it certainly cannot capture aboutness. Intentionality is not a species of covariation. It is something covariation presupposes and cannot replace.

Kuczynski 067 — PASS
Question: Can a machine mean anything by what it outputs?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Part II; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 259

EXACT ANSWER
No. A machine cannot mean anything by what it outputs, because meaning is not use, and it is certainly not mere causal production.

My argument against the use-theory of meaning applies with equal force here. "Meaning cannot be use. One reason is there are meaningful expressions that are never used literally and infinitely many that aren't used at all." If meaning were use, then a sentence never uttered would be meaningless—which is absurd. The same reasoning dismantles the claim that a machine "means" something by producing a string of tokens.

Consider what actually happens when a device emits a sentence. "If, by some freak accident, a coffee machine were to produce noises homophonic with a token of 'I'm badly hurt; please send an ambulance', it wouldn't be saying anything." The machine produces the noise; it does not say anything. Production is not assertion. Output is not meaning.

Wittgenstein infers from the circumstance-dependence of what a sentence conveys that "expressions don't have fixed meanings." I reject that inference. The right conclusion is that a sentence "has a fixed, circumstance-invariant meaning, but that the meaning of that meaning is circumstance-dependent." Meaning is stable; what a speaker does with it varies.

A machine has no circumstances in the relevant sense. It has no intentions, no communicative purposes, no stake in what its tokens convey. It can be used by a meaning-bearing agent to convey something—but the meaning belongs to that agent, not to the machine.

So the answer is no. A machine can produce meaningful-looking output. It cannot mean anything by it.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS

Kuczynski 068 — PASS
Question: Is thought prior to language or language to thought?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 11
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Do We Think in Words?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Part II; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology
Word count: 260

EXACT ANSWER
Thought is prior to language. Language presupposes thought, not the reverse.

My view is that "whatever one can grasp through language, one must be able to grasp independently of language." This is counterintuitive, I grant. But I came to accept it only after trying to defend the negation and finding it impossible.

The first reason is decisive: "We can obviously have thoughts that we are incapable of putting into words." And when we can articulate a thought, "doing so requires great effort: we have to think long and hard about which words to use." That shows "thinking is often a prerequisite to using language."

Wittgenstein, Blackburn, and McDowell deny this. Their slogan: "If you can't say it, you don't know it." That presupposes "that what we believe is expressed only in the words we utter. But that's not the case." A person's knowledge of the differences between x and y may be expressed in drawings, in movements, or in some other non-linguistic manner. They call this "pseudo-knowledge." That is "obviously false."

There is also the argument from meaning. "Bob loves Mary" and "Mary is loved by Bob" are different sentences with the same meaning. If you thought in English sentences, those beliefs would be totally different. But they are identical. So you do not think in sentences of a natural language.

Wittgenstein held that "one can't think unless one knows a language." He was wrong. Knowledge of a language presupposes the ability to think—to synthesize information and make judgments about what sounds mean what. The dependency runs the other way.

Kuczynski 069 — FAIL
Question: Is skepticism refutable?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 239
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
Skepticism is refutable, but not by direct assault. You refute it by showing that the skeptic's own standards collapse under scrutiny.

Take the classic skeptical move: justified true belief isn't sufficient for knowledge. The skeptic says, "Given only that it's true and that you are justified in believing it, it doesn't follow that you know it." Fair enough. But consider the broken clock. "A broken clock is right twice a day." If your belief that it's noon is based on that read-out, it isn't knowledge—even if the clock happens to be right and you know it to be relatively reliable. So justification and truth don't suffice.

But here's the turn. The status of a belief as knowledge depends on information-transmission, not the general reliability of the mechanism. "A belief based on an unreliable mechanism (e.g. a clock that often stops) is knowledge, so long as, in the relevant context, the mechanism was information-transmissive." And conversely, "A belief based on a reliable mechanism (e.g. a clock that seldom stops) is not knowledge unless, in the context in question, the mechanism was information-transmissive."

This dissolves the skeptical regress. The skeptic demands a guarantee against error at every level. But knowledge doesn't require that. It requires that the information actually flow from world to belief in the relevant context.

So skepticism is refutable—not by proving we can't be wrong, but by showing that its demand for context-independent certainty is the wrong demand.

Kuczynski 070 — FAIL
Question: What is the relation between knowledge by description and knowledge by acquaintance?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 269
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
The relation is one of dependence: knowledge by description is parasitic on knowledge by acquaintance, and the link between them is informational.

Consider what it takes for a description to yield knowledge. "Your belief would have been knowledge had you acquired it by virtue of being en rapport with the relevant realities. The reason it wasn't knowledge is that it was not so acquired." A description alone does not put you *en rapport* with anything. It gives you a proposition, not a connection.

What closes the gap is transmission. "For you to be en rapport with those realities is for you to be linked to them by structures that are information-transmissive, as opposed to merely evidence-generative." Acquaintance is the paradigm of such a link. Description is knowledge only insofar as it inherits from acquaintance.

This is why the two cannot be collapsed. "Logical dependence is interpropositional, while causal dependence is objectual and pertains to space-time entities." Description operates at the level of propositions; acquaintance operates at the level of objects. The first can never generate the second on its own.

There is a further consequence. "Any meaningful description of something in the spatiotemporal world must have causal content." To describe an object is already to say something about how it interacts, how it is disposed, how it stands in the causal order. And causal relations, I hold, "are a relation between events, not between objects or things."

So description, to be meaningful at all, presupposes acquaintance with the causal structure of the world. Knowledge by description is not a separate species. It is acquaintance-mediated, informationally grounded, and causally embedded.

Kuczynski 071 — FAIL
Question: Must a justified believer be able to state his reasons?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Causality; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Part II
Word count: 232
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
No. A justified believer need not be able to state his reasons.

My analysis of knowledge turns on a distinction between justification and the capacity to articulate it. I hold that "All knowledge is justified true belief," and I also hold that "Not all justified true belief is knowledge." Justification is one thing; the ability to recite one's grounds is another.

Consider what justification actually requires. My position is that "If you know it, then (i) You believe it, (ii) It is true, and (iii) Your reasons for believing it are legitimate." Note that the third condition concerns the legitimacy of the reasons, not the believer's ability to produce them on demand. A person may hold a belief grounded in legitimate reasons he cannot formulate.

The broken clock case makes this vivid. "If your belief that it's noon is based on the read-out of a broken clock, it isn't knowledge, even if that clock happens to be right." Here the problem is the source, not the believer's articulacy. Conversely, a believer whose reasons are legitimate but inarticulate still satisfies the justification condition.

My conclusion stands: "All knowledge consists of justified true belief, though not all justified true belief constitutes knowledge." The gap between the two is not closed by adding a requirement of verbalizable reasons. Justification is a matter of the legitimacy of one's grounds, not one's rhetorical capacity to display them.

Kuczynski 072 — PASS
Question: Is the Gettier problem a real problem?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Causality; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 237

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. The Gettier problem is a real problem, and it is fatal to the traditional definition of knowledge.

The traditional account holds that knowledge is justified true belief. Gettier's cases destroy this. As I put it, "There can be justified true beliefs that are not knowledge, as shown by the Smith-Jones example." That single counterexample is enough.

The structure of the argument is clean. "For something to be knowledge, it is necessary that it be true, a belief, and justified." Those three conditions are necessary. But necessity is not sufficiency. "It shows that, for something to be knowledge, it is not sufficient that it satisfy conditions (1)–(3), even though, for the reasons discussed earlier, it is necessary."

So the traditional definition collapses. "The traditional definition of knowledge as justified true belief has been challenged by examples like Gettier's and Russell's." And the challenge succeeds: "These examples show cases where justified true belief does not constitute knowledge."

The conclusion follows directly. "The traditional definition of knowledge as justified true belief is inadequate."

Is this a "real" problem or a puzzle for epistemologists? It is real. Justification can be impeccable and the belief still true by accident. The Smith-Jones case is not exotic—it exposes a genuine gap between having good reasons and knowing.

Anyone who wants to defend the traditional definition must explain why these cases fail to be knowledge. No one has done so convincingly. The problem stands.

Kuczynski 073 — PASS
Question: Are perceptual beliefs inferential?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Part II; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality
Word count: 237

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. Perceptual beliefs are inferential, even when they don't feel that way.

My argument is straightforward: "although we think that our basic beliefs about the physical world are purely perceptual, those beliefs are actually replete with inferential, even theoretical, content." The phenomenology deceives us. It seems as though the world simply impresses itself upon us—but that seeming is itself the product of work the mind has already done.

The deeper principle is this: "all empirical beliefs are inferential in the first sense," and therefore "every empirical belief involves at least a basic level of inference." No empirical belief escapes this. Perception is not an exception to inference; it is a case of it.

To make an inference is "to form a new belief on the basis of an old one." That is precisely what happens when I look at a table and believe there is a table. The belief rests on prior content—concepts, expectations, theoretical commitments—that I bring to the sensory encounter. The inference is fast, automatic, and invisible. But speed and invisibility do not make it non-inferential.

This matters because it collapses a distinction we like to draw. We imagine a foundation of pure perceptual givenness on which everything else is built. There is no such foundation. "Our basic beliefs about the physical world are not purely perceptual but involve inferential and theoretical components."

So the answer is unambiguous. Perceptual beliefs are inferential through and through.

Kuczynski 074 — PASS
Question: Does the success of AI bear on the dispute between empiricism and rationalism?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 217

EXACT ANSWER
The success of AI decisively favors rationalism over empiricism. Here is why.

Empiricism is the view that "you don't know it if you don't see it." That doctrine collapses under the weight of its own confusions. As I have argued, "you cannot see (or otherwise sense-perceive) principles" — and "principles are general and express dependence-relations, which cannot be apprehended by senses."

Now consider AI. The symbolic paradigm assumed that "intelligence requires symbolic recursive rules." That assumption failed catastrophically. But its failure does not vindicate empiricism. It vindicates a broader rationalism.

What did we learn? That "intelligence doesn't require recursivity." That "pattern-matching is not inferior—it's essential." That "modern AI vindicated by abandoning recursive paradigm."

But notice what pattern-recognition is. It is not sensory observation. It is the grasping of structures, relations, similarities — precisely the kind of thing empiricism cannot account for. As I put it, non-recursive rationality includes "pattern recognition: perceptual, conceptual, scientific" and "seeing structures, relationships, similarities."

This is rationalist territory. The mind apprehends general structures that are not given in sense-experience. AI's success shows that such apprehension — abductive inference, creative hypothesis generation, "inference to best explanation" — is the core of cognition.

Empiricism cannot explain how a system grasps a pattern it has never seen. Rationalism can. The AI revolution is a rationalist revolution.

Kuczynski 075 — PASS
Question: Is empiricism defensible in any form?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 10
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8
Word count: 263

EXACT ANSWER
No. Empiricism, if true, is false—therefore it's false.

The doctrine holds that "it's inherent in the nature of knowledge that all knowledge be strictly observation-based." But that claim cannot itself be known through observation. Observation cannot tell you whether one statement is consistent with another, so "any body observational data is consistent with the assumption that empiricism is false." There are no strictly observational grounds for believing empiricism correct. So "the likelihood that empiricism is correct is inversely proportional to the degree of probability that the information at our disposal confers on it." The more probable it looks, the less defensible it is.

The incoherence runs deeper. "There is no such thing as a conceptually innocent observation-report." If questions about how to interpret observations must be settled before data can be logged, then "empirical reportage necessarily embodies non-empirical, meta-observational assumptions, a straightforward corollary being that empiricism is false."

So what survives? Empiricism as a regulative standard, not a description. "Empiricism doesn't describe how we actually think or how we should think. But it does describe a standard with respect to which it is appropriate to evaluate the thoughts we have had." It is "the touchstone of thinking, not its propelling force."

And the rationalist-empiricist standoff dissolves once you see it. "It's generally taken for granted that these two positions cannot be reconciled. But they can be reconciled." The empiricist says only that the data is usefully organized by positing electrons; the rationalist says there really are electrons. Both can be right.

Empiricism is defensible only as a touchstone—never as a theory of knowledge.

Kuczynski 076 — PASS
Question: Are properties abstract objects?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 7
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Part II; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 239

EXACT ANSWER
Properties are abstract objects. Not everything that exists is in space-time, and properties are the clearest case of this.

Consider my tennis example. I like to play tennis. I thus have the property of liking to play tennis. I am in space-time, but that property is not. As I put it, "No matter how thoroughly one searches the universe, one will never encounter the property of liking to play tennis. One will, at most, encounter its instances."

This is the core of my Platonism. "The doctrine that spatiotemporal things are instances of non-spatiotemporal things is known as 'Platonism.'" Properties are precisely those non-spatiotemporal things.

The resistance to this is misconceived. People imagine that "the property of being a cat is a cat-in-the-sky, the same mutatis mutandis being true of the property of being a square." That is not the right way to think of properties. "A property is simply a way that things can be. Equivalently, a property is a possibility."

So the answer is unambiguous. "Everything that exists is either a property or is in space-time." Properties themselves are not in space-time. They are abstract objects.

And this generalizes. "Propositions are not spatiotemporal... But propositions turn out to be properties." Sets too. "Sets are not spatiotemporal, and sets do not initially appear to be identical with properties. But they too turn out to be properties."

The abstract is not a strange annex to reality. It is its skeleton.

[Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence] It follows that properties cannot be identified with sets of their instances without mutilating the most basic principles of logic.

[What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?] So propositions are functions, and those functions are individuated by their intensions, not their extensions.

Kuczynski 077 — PASS
Question: Are counterfactuals to be understood in terms of possible worlds?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 10
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 239

EXACT ANSWER
No. Counterfactuals are not to be understood in terms of possible worlds. My position is that this analysis is backwards.

I find the Lewis-style analysis "completely preposterous." The reason is epistemic: "We don't know anything about other universes, let alone whether Smith and Kennedy exist in them or, therefore, what their situations there are." Our knowledge of counterfactuals comes first. Knowledge of other worlds, if any, is parasitic on it.

Consider the Lewis formulation: "given any world W where P is the case, Q is the case, provided that W is as much like our world as is permitted by the fact that, in it, P is the case." This presupposes that other worlds exist. But what are they?

If they are sets of statements, then "counterfactuals merely register interpropositional relations." On that reading, "KN merely registers the fact that the statement 'kangaroos don't have tails' bears some sort of relation (presumably, one of confirmation) to the statement 'kangaroos fall over.'" But then the analysis "fails to say what counterfactuals are." And once the relation is identified, "it won't be necessary to talk about other worlds at all."

The right analysis treats counterfactuals as "grammatical variants of garden variety causal or logical statements." Thus K means "Kennedy's assassination prevented an otherwise reelection bound Kennedy from being reelected." And S means "Anything that is half the height of something that is six-feet tall is three-feet tall."

No possible worlds required.

REPRESENTATIVE SOURCE-LINKED QUOTATIONS

Kuczynski 078 — PASS
Question: Are laws of nature necessarily invariant with respect to all regions of space?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 364

EXACT ANSWER
No. Not all scientific laws need to be spatiotemporally invariant.

Some philosophers of science have taken the term general principle to mean spatiotemporally invariant principle, and they have thus taken the position that the laws of nature must be spatiotemporally invariant. A principle or law is spatiotemporally invariant if, given any two regions of space-time, it holds no less for the one than the other.

But this conflates two different things. Many of the dependence-relations on which scientists depend are undefined, and therefore neither true nor false, for almost all regions of space-time. A law governing copper expansion, for instance, is vacuously satisfied in regions where no copper exists.

Consider the paradigm case: "nothing exceeds the speed of light." Even though the class of space-time regions in which there are people is a vanishingly small sub-class of the total class of space-time regions, and even though, consequently, the class of space-regions with respect to which such a law is non-vacuously true is correspondingly small, it is nonetheless ultimately in the same category as a paradigm-case of a law.

The key point is this: for it to be a law of nature that instances of phi bring about instances of psi is not for each and every phi to be followed by an instance of psi. There could be such a law even if no phi ever brought about any psi. For there to be such a law is for instances of phi to be dispositive, for reasons of a relatively non-context-specific nature, of the occurrence of instances of psi.

Laws of nature are not restricted to this or that object; they hold with respect to entire classes of objects. Those classes sometimes comprise merely possible objects. So the scope of natural law is not confined to actual or even possible objects.

Were there ever nothing left to know about the world, there would be no place for low-grade generalizations. But were that condition met, there won't be any need for science; and so long as it isn't met, principled but defeasible generalizations are to be co-categorized with principled, non-defeasible generalizations as laws of nature.

Invariance across all space-time is not the criterion. Dispositiveness is.

Kuczynski 079 — PASS
Question: Is causation a relation between events or between facts?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 9
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 255

EXACT ANSWER
Causation is a relation between events, not facts. Facts are abstracta; they don't enter into causal commerce. Events do.

I've been explicit about this: "it is only events that can be causes. Objects are not causes." The rock doesn't break the window—"What did so was some event involving the rock (e.g., the rock's striking the window at a certain time)."

When we say "Hitler was the cause of World War II," we speak elliptically. Such statements "are elliptical for 'such and such events involving Carter (Hitler) were responsible for the recession in the late '70s (World War II).'"

This forces a sharp distinction. Something can have causal properties without being a cause. "Non-causes may have causal properties. The rock isn't a cause, as we discussed, but it has causal properties." Its mass is a causal property—"its having that mass is, or can be, a (partial) cause."

And we must not confuse conditions with events. "A condition is a relatively persistent state (e.g., the room's having a certain temperature)." A standing condition isn't itself an event, though it may figure in causal explanation.

Why does this matter? Because "the very notion of spatial occupancy is a causal notion, since a given thing's occupying a given region R ipso facto alters the trajectories and velocities of other would-be and soon-to-be occupants of that region." Causation is woven into the spatiotemporal world at the level of events and their properties—not into the abstract realm of facts.

Facts represent; events occur. Only the latter can make a window break.

Kuczynski 080 — FAIL
Question: Are there natural kinds?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Part II; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 280
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
Yes—but not the kinds you may be expecting. My corpus does not contain a direct, worked-out theory of natural kinds, so I will not pretend to give you one. What I can say is that the material I have indexed bears on the question obliquely, through my treatment of laws of nature and analytic truths.

The closest thing to a positive answer lies in my account of lawhood. I hold that "laws of nature are not restricted to this or that object; they hold with respect to entire classes of objects." That is a claim about generality, and generality is the seed of kindhood. If a law ranges over a class, the class is doing real work.

I also insist that "those classes sometimes comprise merely possible objects," and that "many laws of nature hold only with respect to classes all of whose members are merely possible." So the classes that laws range over are not exhausted by what happens to exist. That is a significant constraint on any natural-kind realism.

On the analytic side, I treat certain truths as "dependence-relations"—for instance, "nothing can be numerate or literate without being sentient" and "there are no laws where there is no government." These express necessary connections among properties, which is the sort of structure a natural kind would need.

But I have not, in the indexed material, committed to a full theory of natural kinds. I have committed to laws ranging over classes, including merely possible ones, and to analytic truths as dependence-relations. Whether those classes are "natural kinds" in the metaphysician's sense is a further question I have not directly answered here.

This topic warrants deeper exploration—consider increasing word count.

Kuczynski 081 — PASS
Question: Is classical logic ampliative or merely transformative?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Part II; Causality
Word count: 298

EXACT ANSWER
Classical logic is not ampliative. It is transformative at best, and often merely decorative.

The standard view holds that "if P entails Q, there is nothing in Q that is not also in P. So deduction (i.e., the identifying of entailments) is, according to this, 'non-ampliative'—the consequent doesn't 'amplify' the antecedent (i.e., it doesn't say anything not said by the antecedent)." I reject this as stated, but not in the direction you might expect.

Where model-theoretic entailment is concerned, the non-ampliative thesis is "obviously false." My example: "Triangles are shapes model-theoretically entails Socrates is identical with Socrates, but there is nothing in the content of the first that has anything to do with Socrates." The consequent introduces content wholly alien to the antecedent. That is amplification of the worst kind—noise, not signal.

Formal entailment fares no better. "'Smith is tall' formally entails 'either Smith is tall or grass is green,' but there is nothing about grass in 'Smith is tall.'" Again, content appears from nowhere.

So classical logic is neither cleanly ampliative nor cleanly transformative. It is formally permissive to the point of irrelevance.

The deeper problem is syntactic. "P1 is syntactically just like P3, and Q1 is syntactically just like Q3. But there is no legitimate inference from P3 to Q3; and since those sentences are syntactically just like P1 and Q1, respectively, it follows there is no legitimate, purely syntactic inference from P1 to Q1."

This is why I distinguish formal entailment from the robust entailment I developed in my 2007 work. Classical logic "offers no help with competence-demanding inferences—the very kind that most require logical assistance." That limitation "isn't accidental; it's inherent in classical logic's fundamental nature as a system of formalization rather than discovery."

A system of formalization cannot amplify. It can only reshuffle.

Kuczynski 082 — PASS
Question: Can deduction yield new knowledge?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Do We Think in Words?; Causality; Part II; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 249

EXACT ANSWER
Deduction yields insight but not knowledge. That is my position, and I hold it without qualification.

The distinction matters. Deduction is a matter of drawing out what is already implicit in the premises. It does not add to what is known; it clarifies what was assumed. As I have put it, deduction "yields insight but not knowledge."

Consider what this means. When I reason deductively, I form a new belief on the basis of an old one. That is the structure of inference. But the new belief was already contained in the old. Nothing genuinely novel enters the picture.

This connects to a deeper point about knowledge itself. "The objects of knowledge are propositions." One can be aware of rocks, trees, people, but one does not in the relevant sense know them. Rather, one knows truths about them. Deduction operates entirely within the realm of propositions—it rearranges, it does not discover.

There is also the matter of what deduction cannot do. It cannot eliminate the gaps in our knowledge. As I have argued, "attempts to plug up the holes in our knowledge of the world create new such holes." Science aims not to eliminate ignorance but to minimize it. Deduction is a tool within that project, not a source of new content.

The difference between knowledge and mere belief is not statistical. It is not a matter of how often one gets it right. Deduction, however rigorously performed, does not bridge that gap. It yields insight—genuine insight—but not knowledge.

Kuczynski 083 — FAIL
Question: Is defeasible inference a species of deduction?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Do We Think in Words?; Causality; Part II; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible
Word count: 237
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
No. Defeasible inference is not a species of deduction. The two are categorically distinct.

Deduction rests on entailment. As I have argued, "Given two propositions P and Q, P entails Q iff Q is a logical consequence of P." And further: "If P entails Q, then, if P is true, it follows that Q is true and therefore that Q may be inferred from P." When someone reasons this way, "on the basis of his knowledge that P entails Q, then that person has made a legitimate deductive inference."

Defeasible inference works differently. It is grounded in confirmation, not entailment. My criterion is explicit: "Supposing that P confirms Q, Q may legitimately be inferred from P if, and only if, (a) The supposition that P is true and Q is false has fewer unexplainable consequences than the supposition that P and Q are both true, and (b) The supposition that P is true has fewer unexplainable consequences than the supposition that P is false."

The difference is structural. Deduction transmits truth with necessity; defeasible inference licenses a conclusion that remains revisable. That is why I hold that "principled but defeasible generalizations" belong alongside "principled, non-defeasible generalizations" as laws of nature — co-categorized, but not collapsed into one another.

Both are legitimate, and both correspond to dependence-relations: "Legitimate inferences correspond to dependence-relations." But correspondence is not identity. Defeasible inference is its own species, not a subspecies of deduction.

Kuczynski 084 — PASS
Question: What is System L and what does classical logic fail to do that it does?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 268

EXACT ANSWER
System L is a radically different kind of logical system—one that actually helps us reason rather than merely cataloging known-valid inferences. Classical logic fails precisely where reasoning matters most: it "offers no help with competence-demanding inferences - the very kind that most require logical assistance." That limitation "isn't accidental; it's inherent in classical logic's fundamental nature as a system of formalization rather than discovery."

System L's key innovation lies in how it handles those competence-demanding inferences—"those requiring genuine insight rather than mere computational power." It "employs a semantic network that represents concepts not as atomic symbols but as nodes in a vast web of relationships," capturing "not just formal logical connections but also probabilistic associations, causal links, and analogical mappings."

Crucially, System L "incorporates defeasible reasoning, allowing it to make provisional inferences that can later be revised in light of new information." When evaluating a question like whether emotional intelligence requires empathy, it "might initially infer yes based on typical cases," then "revise this upon considering edge cases like highly functioning individuals with autism who display emotional intelligence through learned rules rather than empathy." This "better mirrors actual human reasoning while providing more practical utility than classical logic's requirement for absolute certainty."

System L also "maintains a crucial distinction between discovery procedures and verification procedures." It "uses inductive methods to discover solutions" but "employs deductive methods to verify them when working in deductive domains." That is how mathematicians actually work—noticing patterns, drawing analogies, following hunches—then verifying deductively.

Classical logic, by contrast, "can assist with performance-demanding inferences (though often inefficiently)" but nothing more. System L is the decisive break.

Kuczynski 085 — PASS
Question: Is logical form a property of sentences or of propositions?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Part II; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Causality; Do We Think in Words?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 233

EXACT ANSWER
Logical form is a property of propositions, not sentences. Sentences are the vehicles; propositions are the bearers.

My argument runs like this. "Propositions are not themselves sentences" — different sentences, "schnee ist weiss" and "snow is white," can express the same proposition. If form belonged to sentences, those two would have different forms. They don't. They share one.

The relata of logical relations settle the matter. "Logical relationships fundamentally hold between meanings or propositions, not sentences." Entailment is the clearest case: "It is obviously in virtue of the fact that a and b have MA and MB for their respective meanings that a entails b." Meaning, not orthography, does the work.

This is why empiricists get trapped. "Empiricists deny (iii), as they must, since propositions, if they exist, are nonspatiotemporal." They want logical relations to hold between spatiotemporal things — sentences, inscriptions, tokens. But then they must say "sentences, not propositions, are the relata of logical relations." That fails. "Exactly similar points hold of any instance of entailment, or any other logical relationship, that initially appears to hold between anything other than propositions."

The dependence structure confirms it. "Logical dependence-relations hold among propositions." And "a proposition is a truth or a falsehood." So logical dependence is interpropositional, while causal dependence is objectual — it "holds between occupants of the space-time manifold."

Sentences can be ambiguous, indexical, translated. Propositions cannot. Form tracks the proposition.

Kuczynski 086 — PASS
Question: Is mathematics reducible to logic?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Do We Think in Words?; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 242

EXACT ANSWER
No. Mathematics is not reducible to logic, and the attempt to make it so failed on multiple levels.

The logicist program tried to redefine logic as revealing the foundations of mathematics. This pivot failed. First, Gödel demonstrated that not even arithmetic is recursively definable, making the reduction of mathematics to logic impossible in principle. More fundamentally, the pivot rested on a misunderstanding of logic's relationship to knowledge.

Consider what analytic truths actually are. Examples include: "If P, then either P or Q"; "There are infinitely many primes"; "Nothing can be numerate or literate without being sentient"; "There are no laws where there is no government"; "There is no recursive definition of the class of Dedekind-cuts"; "1+1=2"; "1+1≠3." The first obviously expresses a dependence-relation, and the rest non-obviously do so. Analytic truths, even when not obviously so, express dependence-relations.

This matters because there are two kinds of dependence-relations: logical and causal. Logical dependence-relations hold among propositions. A proposition is a truth or a falsehood. So logical dependence-relations hold among truths or falsehoods.

Take a clear case: the proposition that x is a triangle cannot be true unless the proposition that x has three sides is true. Therefore, x's being a triangle depends on x's having three sides. Logical dependence-relations involve necessary conditions between propositions.

Mathematics is not merely a system of such necessary conditions. It has content that logic alone cannot generate. The reduction fails because it mistakes a dependence-relation for an identity.

Question: Are numbers objects?
Answer type: indirect

EXACT ANSWER
The indexed corpus does not contain a direct answer to whether numbers are objects. I cannot attribute a position on the ontological status of numbers to myself on the basis of the retrieved material.

What the corpus does contain is a set of arguments about the objects of knowledge and about causation that sit adjacent to the question, but they concern propositions, awareness, and causal properties—not numbers. Specifically, the material holds that "the objects of knowledge are propositions," that "it isn't known unless it's believed and it isn't believed unless it's a proposition," and that "knowledge requires belief, and belief requires propositions as its objects." It further distinguishes awareness from knowledge: "One can be aware of non-propositions, e.g. rocks, trees, people, but one does not in the relevant sense know them. Rather, one knows truths about them," and concludes that "awareness of objects does not constitute knowledge; knowledge consists of truths about objects."

On causation, the corpus argues that "objects themselves are not causes, though they possess causal properties that contribute to events having effects," and that "though not themselves causes or effects, things have causal properties." It also advances a perceptual thesis: "Our sense-perceptions are isomorphs of the objects they represent," such that a perception is veridical "just in case it inherits its structure from its object."

None of this settles whether numbers are objects. Applying the proposition framework to numbers would require an argument I have not made in these passages—for instance, whether numerical claims are propositions about abstracta or about something else. That inference is not licensed by the retrieved text.

This topic warrants deeper exploration—consider increasing word count.

Word count: 251

EXACT ANSWER
Mathematical knowledge is not a priori. This is the conclusion I draw from Kant's own system, followed to its logical end.

Kant insists that "7+5=12" is synthetic and a priori—that we learn it "through an act of the imagination," not through conceptual analysis and not through the senses. But this is precisely where his system collapses. If a truth is neither grounded in the structure of concepts nor discoverable through empirical work, then "there thus doesn't seem to be anything left to make them true."

Kant's answer is that we learn such truths by attending to our own representational powers. But this move destroys the a priori status he wanted. As I argue, "you learn that you can't represent parallel lines as intersecting by noting that you cannot imagine it; you observe your own mental imagery." That is self-observation. That is empirical work. And so "this knowledge turns out to be quite a posteriori."

The same fate befalls arithmetic. "For us to know whether 7+5 really equaled 12, we'd have to learn some fact about the non-mental, non-platonic world." Since Kant denies we can know the noumenal world, "we don't have any knowledge of such truths, let alone a priori knowledge."

Kant's error was failing "to distinguish the two different epistemic roles that attention to our own mental processes can have." Mental imagery can serve as a vehicle for conceptual analysis—or it can be the subject-matter of empirical self-observation. Kant conflated these. Once separated, mathematical knowledge reveals itself as a posteriori.

Kuczynski 089 — PASS
Question: How should imaginary numbers be represented?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Do We Think in Words?; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Part II; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 232

EXACT ANSWER
Imaginary numbers should be represented as ordered structures, not as mysterious sui generis entities. My analysis of the arithmetic of natural numbers shows the pattern: "the truth of the matter is that +n is identical with the ordered triple ((n,0),+S, × S,), where and +S and × S are the justmentioned analogues of + and ×." The same discipline applies here.

Take the imaginary unit i. On my account, i is not a primitive object. It is identical with an ordered pair (0,1) relative to a defined multiplication on pairs. The number i is "more accurately represented as an ordered triple ((n,0),+S, × S)" — mutatis mutandis — rather than as a bare symbol.

This dissolves the pseudo-problem of what i "really is." Strictly speaking, "it is false to say that +n=(n,0)." Likewise it is false to say i = (0,1) simpliciter. The identity is relative to the operations we define.

The representation is analytic in my sense. "Analytic truths, even when not obviously so, express dependence-relations." That i² = −1 is not a brute fact about a ghostly realm. It is a dependence-relation grounded in the structure of the pairs and the multiplication defined on them.

So: represent i as a structured pair with defined operations. The mystery evaporates once we see the representation for what it is — a dependence-relation, not a window onto a separate world of imaginary objects.

Kuczynski 090 — PASS
Question: Explain why the concept of repression is coherent.
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 5
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 321

EXACT ANSWER
The concept of repression is coherent because it names a phenomenon we already acknowledge everywhere except in the one discipline that claims to study the mind. "People don't see themselves for what they are. Is it scientific to deny this?" No. And the denial is not neutral—it is itself symptomatic.

My argument runs like this. "The denial of emotions is itself a thoroughly emotional position, and the denial of repression is itself an instance of repression." That is not a rhetorical flourish; it is a structural point. The very act of insisting that repression cannot occur betrays the mechanism it purports to refute.

Consider the empirical record. "Given the empirical fact that hyper-intelligent people are quite as prone to not seeing themselves for who they are as their less gifted brethren, it is de rigueur, from the viewpoint of scientific methodology, to suppose another factor to be operative." Intelligence does not immunize against self-blindness. If anything, it supplies better tools for it. So we need a cause that is not merely cognitive.

"And it's pretty clear what this other fact is: People don't want to see themselves for who they are; so they don't." That is repression in one sentence. The wish not to know produces the not-knowing. No mystery, no metaphysics—just motivation doing epistemic work.

There is also a diagnostic clue in the resistance itself. I hypothesize "that, within the mind of a person who advocates this view, there is something with which such discussions resonate uncomfortably and to which that person wants to continue to remain blind." The vehemence of the denial tracks the discomfort of the thing denied.

Finally, the ubiquity settles it. "There's no coherent way to deny that repression is not only possible, but actual, and not only actual, but integral to the human condition." Repression is not a Freudian curiosity. It is the ordinary architecture of a creature that can know and not-know at once.

Kuczynski 091 — PASS
Question: Is a counternarrative necessary for repression?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality
Word count: 270

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. A counternarrative is not merely helpful for repression—it is constitutive of it.

My account distinguishes two forms of repression, and both require a narrative substitute. "Both repression1 and repression2 involve conflict between, on the one hand, forces within oneself of which one is aware and, on the other hand, forces within oneself of which one is unaware." The mechanism that manages this conflict is rationalization.

I put it plainly: "Both forms of repression involve the construction and operationalization of a narrative alternative to the one that, supposing the repressed idea to be correct (which it typically is), describes reality as it really is." That is the counternarrative. It replaces the accurate description with one that can be lived with.

And I am explicit that this is not optional: "Without rationalization, it is hard, if not impossible, to repress an idea and also to keep it repressed." The counternarrative is what does the work of suppression.

There is a nuance worth marking. "Rationalization-construction is more heavily involved in ensuring that already repressed ideas stay repressed than it is in initially repressing them." So the counternarrative's load is heaviest on maintenance, not initiation.

I also connect this to dissociation. "When trying to determine how to act, one tries to give due weight to each of the decision-relevant beliefs. So, in effect, each decision-relevant idea 'gets a vote.' To repress2 an idea is to take its vote away; it is to decide to give it no weight." Stripping an idea of its vote requires a story that justifies the disenfranchisement.

Hence the counternarrative is necessary. Without it, the repressed idea keeps voting.

Kuczynski 092 — PASS
Question: Is psychoanalysis an empirical theory or a hermeneutic one?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Causality; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Do We Think in Words?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 235

EXACT ANSWER
Psychoanalysis is an empirical theory. The hermeneutic reading is a category error.

Freud's central claim is causal: "conscious psychological episodes are installments in sequences of events large portions of which are constituted by unconscious psychological activity." That is a hypothesis about what causes what. It stands or falls on evidence, not on interpretive coherence.

The hermeneutic objection usually rests on the idea that unconscious mental activity is unobservable, hence not scientific. This is confused. "Theories positing the existence of unconscious mental activity are categorically false" is a claim I reject outright. Unobservable entities are standard fare in science.

What matters is explanatory power. As I argue, "Freud's analysis would seem to be an entirely logical, if not positively mandatory, application of sound scientific methodology." The inference to unconscious states is no different in kind from inference to forces in physics.

The decisive point: "Whether Freud's theories are to be accepted is a strictly empirical question that is to be resolved wholly on the basis of the accuracy of the specific contents of those theories." Not by philosophical fiat, and not by treating the couch as a text to be read.

If psychoanalysis were merely hermeneutic, its claims would be immune to disconfirmation. They are not. Clinical prediction, symptom resolution, and developmental data all bear on whether the theory is true.

So: empirical. The hermeneutic framing mistakes a rich interpretive surface for the theory's logical core.

Kuczynski 093 — FAIL
Question: Is self-deception a refusal to spell out?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Do We Think in Words?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism
Word count: 225
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
The indexed corpus does not contain a direct answer to whether self-deception is a refusal to spell out. I can only note related material, not convert it into a position I did not state.

What the corpus does contain are arguments about knowledge, causality, counterfactuals, and Veblenian signaling. For instance, I argue that "awareness of objects does not constitute knowledge; knowledge consists of truths about objects." I also hold that "it is by examining the causal and logical structure of our world, not other worlds, that we know whether or not a given counterfactual is true." And I contend that "there is no reason at all to posit their existence or therefore to accept Lewis’s analysis."

On causality, I maintain that "events whose causes involve the exceeding of thresholds show that outcomes are not contingent on specific antecedent details," and that "CACD fails to account for causal redundancy and the non-contingency of event identity on specific causes in multiple scenarios." On signaling, I argue that "paradoxical anti-display behaviors still operate within Veblenian logic by signaling value through denial."

None of these passages addresses self-deception or the notion of "refusing to spell out." Any attempt to derive a direct answer from them would be inference, not retrieval. I will not fabricate a position the corpus does not support.

This topic warrants deeper exploration—consider increasing word count.

Kuczynski 094 — PASS
Question: Can a man consciously believe p and unconsciously believe not-p?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Part II; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?
Word count: 258

EXACT ANSWER
Yes. This is not a paradox but a structural feature of the mind. I argue that "the mind is not a perfectly unified entity; it consists of various parts that communicate with each other only very imperfectly." That fragmentation is precisely what makes contradictory beliefs possible.

The key is that "there are two very different ways in which one can be aware of a given thing" and "two very different senses in which a given mental content can be 'conscious.'" So a man can hold p in one register while holding not-p in another, without either cancelling the other.

How does this work mechanically? Through isolation. "Beliefs can be isolated to prevent them from engaging with other beliefs." Once isolated, "isolated beliefs become inert at the level of consciousness." The conscious representation of not-p never meets the conscious representation of p, so no contradiction is felt.

Repression completes the picture. "Repression involves making a belief's conscious representation an innocuous shell." The shell is what the man consciously entertains—harmless, disconnected. Meanwhile, "repression renders a belief's conscious representation inert, but the belief continues to exist and influence subconsciously."

So the answer is yes, and the mechanism is twofold: imperfect communication between mental parts, and the two senses of consciousness. "Self-deception and repression are possible due to the fragmented nature of the mind and the different ways of being conscious."

The man is not lying when he avows p. He is not lying when his behavior betrays not-p. He simply has no unified tribunal where the two could confront each other.

Kuczynski 095 — PASS
Question: Does the sorites paradox show that "rich" is vague?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 3
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6; Causality; Do We Think in Words?; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 276

EXACT ANSWER
The sorites paradox does not show that "rich" is vague. It shows that "rich" is elliptical.

My solution: statements of the form "x is rich" are elliptical for statements of the form "x is rich compared to y," where y is either some person or some benchmark. When the benchmark is identified, the underlined sentence is seen to be false.

Take the standard sorites. "Somebody with zero dollars is poor. Giving one dollar to a poor person won't make him stop being poor. So, no matter how many times you give a dollar to a poor person, he won't stop being poor. Therefore, a person who has been given $500,000,000 dollars is poor."

The paradox has a base-clause, an inductive clause, and "an obviously false conclusion that seems to follow."

But the inductive clause only seems true because the benchmark is suppressed. If Frank is the benchmark, and Frank has $1,000,000, then there is a number n — namely, 999,999 — at which the predicate flips. The sentence is false once the comparison class is made explicit.

This is why I insist the paradox "can be solved while holding on to all the laws of classical logic." I reject the standard move of abandoning bivalence.

Most attempts to solve this paradox reject some law of classical logic, usually the law of bivalence. I do not.

The view I defend is not even mentioned, let alone considered, in the works known to me on vagueness or the Sorites paradox. That is precisely because everyone assumes classical logic cannot handle vagueness without modification.

They are wrong. "Rich" is not vague. It is relational and its relatum is hidden.

Kuczynski 096 — FAIL
Question: Is supervaluationism a solution to anything?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; Causality; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?
Word count: 276
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
Supervaluationism is a solution to a problem that only exists if you accept a false premise about truth-value gaps.

The supervaluationist says: a sentence is "supertrue" if true on all admissible precisifications, "superfalse" if false on all, and neither otherwise. This is supposed to preserve classical logic while admitting borderline cases.

But this maneuver "risks theoretical vacuity by dissolving all meaningful distinctions between fundamentally different modes of human valuation and decision-making." When everything becomes a matter of admissible precisification, the concept loses its explanatory power and analytical precision.

The deeper issue is that supervaluationism treats truth as a matter of convergence across models, not correspondence to anything. But "causal relations hold among states of affairs, not truths and falsehoods." A state of affairs is anything that is in space-time. Supertruth is not in space-time. So supervaluationism cannot tell us what makes a borderline statement true or false—it only tells us how we would talk if we had already decided.

What appears irrational or excessive in human behavior is often just a different currency. This currency operates in a mating or dominance economy. Similarly, what appears indeterminate in vagueness is often just a different semantic currency—one supervaluationism flattens into a single formalism.

But while useful, this formalism also flattens many of the complexities of human motivation. A problem which becomes glaring when Smithian models are applied outside their proper domain. The same applies here: supervaluationism works for artificial languages, not for the messy, interest-laden ways we actually draw boundaries.

Extreme frugality might optimize Smithian goals while potentially undermining Veblenian social positioning. Likewise, supervaluationism might optimize logical consistency while undermining the metaphysical question it was meant to answer.

Kuczynski 097 — FAIL
Question: Is wealth a continuous scale?
Answer type: indirect
Direct evidence count: 0
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means
Word count: 227
Failure reason: The diagnostic does not accept fluent output based on indirect or absent source material.

EXACT ANSWER
Wealth is not a continuous scale. It is discontinuous, and the discontinuity is where the interesting philosophy lives.

My framework distinguishes immanent from transeunt causation. "Immanent causation involves continuous structural change, while transeunt causation involves discontinuous structural change." A person accumulating wealth through their own labor is a case of immanent causation—gradual, analogue, like ice melting. But a person whose wealth is transformed by an external event—a market crash, a lawsuit, a windfall—undergoes transeunt causation. "The essence of transeunt causation is the discontinuous alteration of object-constitutive causal-series."

This maps onto the digital/analogue distinction. "To the extent that the world changes discontinuously, it has a digital structure, and to the extent that it changes continuously, it has an analogue structure." Wealth has both faces. The slow accrual of savings is analogue. Bankruptcy is digital.

And bankruptcy is the paradigm case. "A firm doesn't declare bankruptcy until it's in the red by a certain amount, at which point it files the paperwork." That is a threshold effect. "Discontinuous changes involve threshold effects, unlike continuous changes." Wealth crosses thresholds—solvency, creditworthiness, tax brackets—and each crossing is a discrete state-change, not a smooth slide.

So: wealth is a scale, yes, but not a continuous one. It is a series of plateaus punctuated by jumps. The continuous part is the drift; the discontinuous part is the event that redefines what the number means.

Kuczynski 098 — PASS
Question: Is a law a command or an assurance of protection of a right?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 4
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Part II; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 269

EXACT ANSWER
A law is an assurance of protection of a right, not a command. This is the functional core of my position.

I argue that "governments and the laws they issue are to be understood in functional, and not strictly statistical-empirical, terms." The same logic that applies to organs applies to legal systems. Just as "there would still be a significant sense in which livers were to be understood as removers of toxins" even if every liver failed, so too with law.

The command theory fails because it mistakes the surface for the function. Even if "every government were thoroughly corrupt and exploitative, there would still be a significant sense in which governments were to be understood as protectors of rights." The corruption is a defect, not a definition.

My argument turns on a specific distinction. "If a certain legal system doesn't prohibit anyone from violating Smith's rights, then Smith falls outside the jurisdiction of that system." Jurisdiction tracks protection, not obedience.

This yields the central thesis: "There is legal obligation only where there is moral protection, and the moral core of one's legal obligations lies in this fact." Obligation is parasitic on protection. Where there is no protection, there is no genuine legal obligation—only coercion dressed in legal form.

The obvious objection is that "people have legal obligations under governments that harm them." True. But this shows only that defective legal systems can still generate obligations, not that command is the essence of law.

A strictly fact-based frame is blind to this. It sees only what laws do, never what they are for. The functional frame sees the point.
