Kuczynski 099 — PASS
Question: Is adjudication covert legislation?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 2
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Causality; Part II; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology   Volume 8; Do We Think in Words?; Why Does Alcoholics Anonymous Work?; Properties and Non-Spatiotemporal Existence
Word count: 226

EXACT ANSWER
No. Adjudication is interpretation, and interpretation is answerable to objective standards.

Blackstone said that judges identify, and do not create, law. John Austin described Blackstone's position as a "childish fiction." Given the argument just outlined—and, in fact, independently of that argument—we must agree with Austin. Judges do not merely find pre-existing law lying around like buried treasure.

But agreeing with Austin does not commit me to the opposite error. We must disagree with the nihilistic view, held by the so-called "legal realists," that judicial interpretation is mere legislation, and isn't answerable to objective standards.

So some interpretations of law are truer to existing law than others. That is the whole point. If adjudication were covert legislation, the phrase "truer to existing law" would be empty—there would be nothing for an interpretation to be true *to*.

The realist conflates two things: the fact that judges exercise judgment with the claim that judgment is unconstrained. It is not. Interpretation operates within a space of reasons set by the law itself.

This is why I reject both poles. Blackstone's fiction denies the creative element. The realists' nihilism denies the constraining element. Both miss that adjudication is a *normative* activity—answerable, defeasible, and corrigible.

The honest position: judges make law at the margins, but they do so under the discipline of existing law. That discipline is what distinguishes adjudication from legislation.

Kuczynski 100 — PASS
Question: Is philosophy a matter of argument or of insight?
Answer type: direct_aligned
Direct evidence count: 1
Representative quotation count: 20
Named works: Empiricism and the Foundations of Psychology; Libet’s Experiment   Why It Matters and What It Means; Causality; Do We Think in Words?; Chapter 27   How Is Rationalization Possible; Berkeley and Contemporary Anti foundationalism; What Are Propositions and What Is Truth?; Russell's Improvements on Frege's Work   Chapter 6
Word count: 281

EXACT ANSWER
Philosophy is a matter of insight, and argument is one of its instruments—not its goal.

My analysis of the square-circle case makes this plain. When someone says "the square is not a circle," the naive reading treats this as a claim about some entity. But that reading is confused. What the statement actually does is deny coinstantiation: "these two properties don't have any instances in common—that they are not coinstantiated."

So the statement "attribute[s] properties to properties." It says that "the property of being both a square and a circle is uninstantiated." That is the insight. The argumentative machinery merely delivers it.

This is why I insist that "in at least some cases, philosophical insight is acquired, not by doing parascience, but by analyzing meanings—not by positing entities, but by clarifying statements." The insight is the destination; the analysis is the road.

The same structure recurs across my work. Knowledge is not awareness of objects but of truths: "One can be aware of non-propositions, e.g. rocks, trees, people, but one does not in the relevant sense know them. Rather, one knows truths about them." That is an insight about the shape of knowledge, not a deduction from premises.

Even in the sciences, insight governs. "Observations are objectual awarenesses," but "the contents of such awarenesses must be converted into propositions if they are to be explained." The conversion is argumentative work; the recognition that it must occur is insight.

And on laws: "principled but defeasible generalizations... are to be co-categorized with principled, non-defeasible generalizations... as laws of nature." That is a reclassification, a seeing-as, not a proof.

Argument without insight is parascience. Insight without argument is mute. But the aim is insight.
